# Indigenous Australians

This article is about the two ethnically distinct groups of indigenous peoples of Australia: [Aboriginal Australians](2912594.md) and [Torres Strait Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islanders). For the whole Australian population, see [Australians](11197621.md).

**Indigenous Australians** are the various [Aboriginal Australian](2912594.md) peoples of Australia, and the ethnically distinct people of the [Torres Strait Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islands). The terms ***Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples***, ***First Nations of Australia***, ***First Peoples of Australia***, and ***First Australians*** are also common. Many Indigenous Australians prefer to identify with their specific cultural group.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommon_Ground-3][^cite_note-6]

Estimates from the [2021 Australian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Australian_census) show that there were almost one million Indigenous Australians, representing 3.8% of the Australian population. About 92% of Indigenous Australians identified as Aboriginal, 4% identified as Torres Strait Islander and 4% identified with both groups.[^cite_note-auto1-1] About 84% spoke English at home and 9% spoke an Aboriginal or Torres Strait language at home. Just over half hold secular or other spiritual beliefs or no religious affiliation; about 40% are Christian; and about 1% adhere to a traditional Aboriginal religion.

The ancestors of Aboriginal Australians moved into what is now the Australian continent about 50,000 to 65,000 years ago, during the [last glacial period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period), arriving by [land bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_bridge) and short sea crossings from what is now Southeast Asia. The ancestors of today's [Torres Strait Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islanders) arrived from what is now [Papua New Guinea](22943.md) around 2,500 years ago and settled the islands on the northern tip of the Australian landmass.

Aboriginal Australians were complex [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer) with diverse economies and societies. There were about 600 tribes or nations and 250 languages with various dialects.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlood201921–22,_37-7][^cite_note-Broome-2019a22-8] Certain groups engaged in [fire-stick farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire-stick_farming)[^cite_note-\:462-9] and [fish farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_farming)[^cite_note-\:472-10] and built [semi-permanent shelters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_architecture#Australia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlood2019239–40-11][^cite_note-\:110-12] The extent to which some groups engaged in agriculture is controversial.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlood201925–27,_146-13][^cite_note-\:502-14][^cite_note-\:512-15] Torres Strait Islanders were seafarers and obtained their livelihood from seasonal [horticulture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horticulture) and the resources of their reefs and seas. They also developed agriculture on some of their islands. Villages had appeared in their areas by the 14th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVethO'Connor201334–35-16][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlood2019198–99-17]

The Indigenous population prior to British settlement in 1788 has been estimated from 318,000[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_1301.02002-18] to more than 3,000,000.[^cite_note-\:3-19] A population collapse, principally from new infectious diseases, followed British colonisation in 1788.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBallyn2011-20][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlynnJenifer2004145–146-21] [Massacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_massacres_of_Indigenous_Australians), [frontier armed conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_frontier_wars) and competition over resources with British settlers also contributed to the decline of the Aboriginal population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvansØrsted-Jensen201428-22][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC_News2017-23]

From the 1930s, the Indigenous population began to recover and Indigenous communities founded organisations to advocate for their rights. From the 1960s Indigenous people won the right to vote in federal and state elections, and some won the return of parts of their traditional lands. In 1992 the [High Court of Australia](314201.md), in the [Mabo Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mabo_v_Queensland_\(No_2\)), found that Indigenous native title rights existed in [common law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_law). By 2021 Indigenous Australians had exclusive or shared title to about 54% of the Australian land mass.[^cite_note-\:632-24] Since 1995 the [Australian Aboriginal flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_flag) and the [Torres Strait Islander flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islander_flag) have been official [flags of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Australian_flags).

From the 19th to the mid-20th century, government policy [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_Generations) many [mixed heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-caste) children from Aboriginal communities, with the intent to assimilate them to what had become the majority white culture. In 1997 the [Australian Human Rights Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Human_Rights_Commission) found that the policy constituted genocide.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBringing_them_Home:_"Chapter_13"1997\[httpshumanrightsgovauour-workbringing-them-home-chapter-13Heading103_'Genocide'\]-25][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESentance2020-26]

## Terminology



The [Australian Aboriginal flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_flag), designed by [Harold Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Thomas_\(artist\)). Together with the [Torres Strait Islander flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islander_flag) below, it was proclaimed a flag of Australia in 1995.

### Variations

There are a number of contemporary appropriate terms to use when referring to Indigenous peoples of Australia. In contrast to when settlers referred to them by various terms, in the 21st century there is consensus that it is important to respect the "preferences of individuals, families, or communities, and allow them to define what they are most comfortable with" when referring to Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommon_Ground-3]

The word *[aboriginal](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/aboriginal)* has been in the English language since at least the 16th century to mean "first or earliest known, indigenous". It comes from the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) *ab* (from) and *origo* (origin, beginning).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAborigine:_Merriam-Webster.com_Dictionary-27] The term was used in Australia as early as 1789 to describe its [Aboriginal peoples](2912594.md). It became capitalised and was used as the common term to refer to both Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islanders. Today, the latter peoples are not included in this term. The term "Aborigine" (as opposed to "Aboriginal") is often disfavoured, as it is regarded as having [colonialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Australia) connotations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommon_Ground-3][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorff2021a-28][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together2020-29]

While the term "Indigenous Australians" has grown in popularity since the 1980s,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlinders_University:_Appropriate_Terminology-30] many Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples dislike it. They feel that it is too generic and removes their distinct clan and people identity. However, many people think that the term is useful and convenient, and can be used where appropriate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommon_Ground-3][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAIATSIS2015-5] Since the early 2000s terms such as *First Nations, First Peoples* and *First Australians* have become more common as the emphasise the sovereignty of their people and the nations prior to colonisation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommon_Ground-3][^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32]

Being as specific as possible, for example naming the [language group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_languages) (such as [Arrernte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrernte_people)), or [demonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonym) relating to geographic area (such as [Nunga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nunga)), is preferred as a way to affirm and maintain a sense of identity.[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34] An example of this is a speech given by [Rosalie Kunoth-Monks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosalie_Kunoth-Monks) is which she stated:[^cite_note-35]

> I am not an Aboriginal, or indeed Indigenous. I am an Arrernte, Alyawarra First Nations person, a sovereign person from this country (speaks in language).

— Rosalie Kunoth-Monks, via [Q+A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Q+A_\(Australian_talk_show\)), 9 June 2014

#### Terms "Black" and "Blackfella"

See also: [Australian Aboriginal identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_identity)

British colonists from their early settlement used the term "Black" to refer to Aboriginal Australians[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Hobart_Town_Courier''18281-36] and later Torres Strait Islanders. While the term originally related to [skin colour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skin_colour) and was often used pejoratively,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommon_Ground-3] today the term is used to indicate Aboriginal or Torres Strait Islander heritage or culture in general. It refers to any people of such heritage regardless of their skin pigmentation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBird2011-37]

In the 1970s, with a rise in Aboriginal activism, leaders such as [Gary Foley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Foley) proudly embraced the term "Black". For example, writer [Kevin Gilbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Gilbert_\(author\))'s book of that time was entitled *Living Black*. The book included interviews with several members of the Aboriginal community, including [Robert Jabanungga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Jabanungga), who reflected on contemporary Aboriginal culture.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilbert1977-38] *[Living Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Living_Black)* is also the name of an Australian TV news and current affairs program covering "issues affecting Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Australians".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENITV:_Living_Black2015-39] It is presented and produced by [Karla Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karla_Grant), an [Arrernte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arrernte_people) woman.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEANZSOG:_Karla_Grantn.d.-40]

Use of the term "Black" varies depending on context, and its use may be deemed inappropriate.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommon_Ground-3] Furthermore, the term sometimes causes confusion as it can refer to Indigenous Australians, other groups such as [African Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Australians) and Melanesian Australians ([South Sea Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sea_Islanders), [Papua New Guinean Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papua_New_Guinean_Australians) and [Fijian Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fijian_Australians)), or all [Black people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_people).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuantai2015-41]

A significant number of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people use the term "[Blackfella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackfella)" and its associated forms to refer to Aboriginal Australians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommon_Ground-3][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELatimore2021-42][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQUoT:_Guidelines2015-43]

#### "Blak"

See also: [Blak sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blak_sovereignty)

The term *blak* is sometimes used as part of a wider social movement (seen in terms such as "**Blaktivism**" and "Blak History Month"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlak_History_Monthn.d.-44]). The term was coined in 1991 by photographer and multimedia artist [Destiny Deacon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destiny_Deacon), in an exhibition entitled *Blak lik mi*. For Deacon's 2004 exhibition at the [Museum of Contemporary Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museum_of_Contemporary_Art_Australia), *blak* was defined in a museum guide as: "a term used by some Aboriginal people to reclaim historical, representational, symbolical, stereotypical and romanticised notions of Black or Blackness. Often used as ammunition or inspiration."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMunroMcLisky2004-45] Deacon said that removing the *c* from *black* to "de-weaponise the term 'black [cunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cunt)'"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBalla2021-46] was "taking on the 'colonisers' language and flipping it on its head".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMunro2020-47]

Contemporary Aboriginal arts in the 21st century are sometimes referred to as a "Blak" arts movement, expressed in names such as BlakDance,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoehme2017-48] BlakLash Collective,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlakLash_Creativen.d.-49] and the title of [Thelma Plum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thelma_Plum)'s song and album, *[Better in Blak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Better_in_Blak)*. Melbourne has an annual Blak & Bright literary festival,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlak_&_Brightn.d.-50] Blak Dot Gallery, Blak Markets, and Blak Cabaret.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMunro2020-47]

#### Aboriginal Australians



A group of Aboriginal men in [possum-skin cloaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Possum-skin_cloak) (c. 1858) in [Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_Victorians)



Men and boys playing a game of *gorri*, 1922

Main articles: [Aboriginal Australians](2912594.md), [List of Australian Aboriginal group names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Australian_Aboriginal_group_names), and [Aboriginal Australians § Groups and sub-groups](2912594.md#Groups_and_sub-groups)

"Aboriginal Australians" refers to the various peoples indigenous to mainland Australia and associated islands, excluding the Torres Strait Islands.

The term Aboriginal Australians includes many regional groups that may be identified under names based on local language, locality, or what they are called by neighbouring groups. Groups can overlap, contain sub-groups. Groups can also evolve over time, and significant changes have occurred since colonisation. The word "community" is often used to describe groups identifying by [kinship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_kinship), [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_language), or belonging to a particular place or "country". An individual community may identify itself by many names, each of which can have alternative English spellings.

Throughout the history of the continent, there have been many different Aboriginal groups, each with its own individual [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_languages), culture, and belief structure. At the time of British settlement, there were over 200 distinct languages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeath2017-51]

#### Torres Strait Islanders

Main article: [Torres Strait Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islanders)



[Torres Strait Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islands)



A 1960 photograph of native inhabitants of [Murray Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murray_Island).

Torres Strait Islanders are the Indigenous Melanesian people of the [Torres Strait Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islands), which are part of the state of [Queensland](59051.md). They are ethnically distinct from the Aboriginal peoples of the rest of Australia. Six percent of Indigenous Australians identify fully as [Torres Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait) Islanders. A further 4% of Indigenous Australians identify as having both Torres Strait Islander and Aboriginal heritage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS2004a-52]

The [Torres Strait Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islands) comprise over 100 islands,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlaces_–_Torres_Strait_Islands2005-53] which were annexed by Queensland in 1879.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlaces_–_Torres_Strait_Islands2005-53]

### Other groupings

"Freshwater people" redirects here. For the American people, see [Freshwater tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freshwater_tribe).

Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people also sometimes refer to themselves by descriptions that relate to their [ecological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological) environment, such as **saltwater people** for coast-dwellers (including Torres Strait Islander people[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArt_Gallery_of_NSW2014-55]),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOgden2013-56][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmythMonaghanSmythConsultants2004-57][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReef_Catchmentsn.d.-58][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllmonKelly2018-59][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcNiven2004329–349-60] **freshwater people**,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC_News2015-61][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAHRC:_Nulungu_Reconciliation_Lecture2014-62] **rainforest people**,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFerrier2015-63][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGregor2016-64][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarper2017-65] **desert people**,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETourism_Australia2020-66][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAboriginal_Australian_Art_&_Culturen.d.-67][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIndigenous_Desert_Alliance-68] or **spinifex people**,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAIATSIS2015-5] (the latter referring to the [Pila Nguru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pila_Nguru) of [Western Australia](33613.md)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECasey2009122–138-69][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELoxley2002-70]

## History

Main articles: [History of Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Indigenous_Australians), [Prehistory of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_Australia), and [Australian archaeology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_archaeology)

See also: [Aboriginal Australians § Origins](2912594.md#Origins), and [List of Indigenous Australian historical figures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indigenous_Australian_historical_figures)

### Migration to Mainland Australia and Torres Strait Islands



Artwork depicting the first contact that was made with the [Gweagal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gweagal) Aboriginal people and [Captain James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captain_James_Cook) and his crew on the shores of the [Kurnell Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cronulla_sand_dunes), New South Wales

The ancestors of [Aboriginal Australians](2912594.md) moved into what is now [Oceania](22621.md) about 50,000 to 65,000 years ago,[^cite_note-\:02-71][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarksonJacobsMarwickFullagar2017-72][^cite_note-Veth-73][^cite_note-FaganDurrani20182-74] during the [last glacial period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Glacial_Period), arriving by [land bridges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_bridge) and short sea crossings from what is now Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-Oppenheimer2013-75]

The first phase of occupation of the Torres Strait Islands began about 4,000 years ago. By 2,500 years ago more of the islands were occupied and a distinctive [Torres Strait Islander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islanders) maritime culture emerged. Agriculture also developed on some islands and by 700 years ago villages appeared.[^cite_note-\:44-76]

Several settlements of humans in Australia have been dated around 49,000 years ago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarcus2016-77][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC2016a-78] [Luminescence dating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luminescence_dating) of sediments surrounding stone artefacts at [Madjedbebe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madjedbebe), a rock shelter in northern Australia, indicates human activity as early as 65,000 years BP.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarksonJacobsMarwickFullagar2017306–310-79] Genomic studies, however, suggest that the main wave of modern humans into Australia ancestral to Aboriginal Australians happened as recently as 37,000 to 50,000 years ago.[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-auto6-81][^cite_note-\:2-82] Accordingly, earlier groups either went extinct or contributed around \~2% ancestry to modern Aboriginal Australians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMalaspinasWestawayMullerSousa2016207–214-83][^cite_note-84][^cite_note-85][^cite_note-86] Indigenous Australians and other Oceanians were probably part of the same [southern route dispersal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Dispersal) as the ancestors of [Ancient Ancestral South Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetics_and_archaeogenetics_of_South_Asia), [Andamanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andamanese), and [East Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_East_Asians).[^cite_note-\:0-87][^cite_note-\:1-88][^cite_note-\:2-82][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYang2022-89][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipsonReich2017-90]

The earliest anatomically modern human remains found in Australia (and outside of Africa) are those of [Mungo Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mungo_Man); they have been dated at 42,000 years old.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowlerJohnstonOlleyPrescott2003-91][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOlleyRobertsYoshidaBowler20062469–2474-92] The initial comparison of the [mitochondrial DNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitochondrial_DNA) from the skeleton known as *Lake Mungo 3* (LM3) with that of ancient and modern Aboriginal peoples indicated that Mungo Man is not related to Australian Aboriginal peoples.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdcockDennisEastealHuttley2001537–542-93] The sequence has been criticised as there has been no independent testing, and it has been suggested that the results may be due to posthumous modification and thermal degradation of the DNA.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECooper20011655–1656-94][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESmithChamberlainRileyStringer2003203–217-95][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Museum-96][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEForum2001163-97] Although the contested results seem to indicate that Mungo Man may have been an extinct subspecies that diverged before the most recent common ancestor of contemporary humans,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdcockDennisEastealHuttley2001537–542-93] the administrative body for the [Mungo National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mungo_National_Park) believes that present-day local Aboriginal peoples are descended from the [Lake Mungo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Mungo) remains.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMungo_National_Park-98]

It is generally believed that Aboriginal people are the descendants of a single migration into the continent, a people that split from the ancestors of East Asians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMallickLiLipsonMathieson2016-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipsonReich2017-90]

Recent work with mitochondrial DNA suggests a founder population of between 1,000 and 3,000 women to produce the genetic diversity observed, which suggests that "initial colonisation of the continent would have required deliberate organised sea travel, involving hundreds of people".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliam2013-100] Aboriginal people seem to have lived a long time in the same environment as the now extinct [Australian megafauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_megafauna).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUoS2005-101]

### Genetics

Further information: [Aboriginal Australians § Origins](2912594.md#Origins), and [Ancient East Eurasians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_East_Eurasians)



Phylogenetic position of the Aboriginal Australian lineage among other [East Eurasians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East-Eurasian)

Genetic studies have suggested that Aboriginal Australians largely descended from an [Eastern Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_East_Eurasians) population wave during the [Initial Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Initial_Upper_Paleolithic), and are most closely related to other [Oceanians](22621.md), such as [Melanesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanesians). The Aboriginal Australians also show affinity to [Ancient Ancestral South Indians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetics_and_archaeogenetics_of_South_Asia), the [Andamanese people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andamanese_people), as well as to [East Asian peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian_peoples). Phylogenetic data suggests that an early initial eastern non-African (ENA) or East-Eurasian meta-population trifurcated, and gave rise to Australasians (Oceanians), the Ancient Ancestral South Indians, [Andamanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onge) and the East/Southeast Asian lineage including the ancestors of [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas),[^cite_note-\:1-88][^cite_note-\:2-82][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYang2022-89][^cite_note-\:0-87] although Papuans may have also received some geneflow from an earlier group (xOOA) as well, around 2%,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECulottaGibbons2016-102] next to additional archaic admixture in the [Sahul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahul) region.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEYang2022-89][^cite_note-103][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETaufikTeixeiraLlamasSudoyo2022-104]



PCA of Orang Asli (Semang) and Andamanese, with worldwide populations in HGDP

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAghakhanianYunusNaiduJinam20151206–1215-105]

Rasmussen et al. 2011 shows that Aboriginal Australians have a lower proportion of European alleles compared to Asians, which they believe is indicative of a multiple dispersal model.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEICommunity_Newsletter2012-106] Genetically, while Aboriginal Australians are most closely related to [Melanesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanesians) (including [Papuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_people_of_New_Guinea)) people, McEvoy et al. 2010[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcEvoyLindWangMoyzis2010297–305-107] believed there is also another component that could indicate Ancient Ancestral South Indian admixture or more recent European influence. Research indicates a single founding [Sahul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_\(continent\)) group with subsequent isolation between regional populations which were relatively unaffected by later migrations from the Asian mainland, which may have introduced the [dingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dingo) 4,000–5,000 years ago. The research also suggests a divergence from the Papuan people of [New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guinea) and the [Mamanwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamanwa) people of the [Philippines](23440.md) about 32,000 years ago, with a rapid population expansion about 5,000 years ago.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcEvoyLindWangMoyzis2010297–305-107] A 2011 genetic study found evidence that the Aboriginal, Papuan and Mamanwa peoples carry some of the alleles associated with the [Denisovan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denisovan) peoples of Asia, (not found amongst populations in mainland Asia) suggesting that modern and archaic humans interbred in Asia approximately 44,000 years ago, before Australia separated from New Guinea and the migration to Australia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERasmussenGuoWangLohmueller201194–98-108][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECallaway2011-109] A 2012 paper reports that there is also evidence of a substantial genetic flow from India to northern Australia estimated at slightly over four thousand years ago, a time when changes in tool technology and food processing appear in the Australian archaeological record, suggesting that these may be related.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEManfredMarkIrinaFrederick20131803–1808-110] Bergström et al 2016 and Nagle et al 2016 could not replicate the 2012 study, noting that Indigenous Australians split from mainland Asia 50,000 years ago.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112] Mallick et al. 2016 and Mark Lipson et al. 2017 study found that the bifurcation of Eastern Eurasian and Western Eurasian dates back to least 45,000 years ago, with Australasians nested inside the Eastern Eurasian clade.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMallickLiLipsonMathieson2016-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELipsonReich2017-90] Vallini et al. 2024 noted that the divergence between Ancient East Eurasians and West Eurasians most likely occurred on the [Persian Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_Plateau) >48,000 years ago, with East Eurasians dispersing throughout the Asia-Pacific region >45,000 years ago.[^cite_note-\:2-82]

#### Uniparentals

Aboriginal Australian men have [haplogroup C-M347](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_C-B477) in high frequencies with peak estimates ranging from 60.2%[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHudjashovKivisildUnderhillEndicott20078726–8730-113] to 68.7%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKayserBrauerWeissSchiefenhövel2003281–302-114] In addition, the basal form K2\* (K-M526) of the extremely ancient [haplogroup K2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_K-M526) – whose subclades [haplogroup R](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_R_\(Y-DNA\)), [haplogroup Q](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_Q-M242), [haplogroup M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_M-P256) and [haplogroup S](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_S-B254) can be found in the majority of Europeans, South Asians, [Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas) and the [Indigenous peoples of Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_Oceania) – has only been found in living humans today amongst Aboriginal Australians. 27% of them may carry K2\* and approximately 29% of Aboriginal Australian males belong to subclades of [K2b1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_K2b1_\(Y-DNA\)), also known as [M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_M-P256) and [S](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_S-B254).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagleBallantynevan_OvenTyler-Smith2015367–381-115]

Aboriginal Australians possess deep rooted clades of both mtDNA [haplogroup M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_M_\(mtDNA\)) and [haplogroup N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haplogroup_N_\(mtDNA\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacAulayHillAchilliRengo20051034–1036-116]

### Before European contact

#### Aboriginal people



Aboriginal Australians, from [Ridpath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Clark_Ridpath)'s *Universal History*

Aboriginal people lived as [foragers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foragers) and [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer). Although Aboriginal society was generally mobile, or [semi-nomadic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomadic), moving according to the changing food availability found across different areas as seasons changed, the mode of life and material cultures varied greatly from region to region. The greatest population density was to be found in the southern and eastern regions of the continent, the [River Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Murray) valley in particular.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPascoe2018-117]



The [scarred tree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scarred_tree) is where [Aboriginal](2912594.md) people have removed the bark to make a canoe, for use on the [Murray River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murray_River). It was identified near [Mildura, Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mildura,_Victoria), and is now in the Mildura Visitor Centre.

There is some evidence that, before outside contact, some groups of Aboriginal Australians had a complex subsistence system that was only recorded by the first European explorers. One early settler took notes on the life styles of the [Wathaurung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wathaurong) people whom he lived near in Victoria. He saw women harvesting [murnong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murnong) tubers, a native yam that is now almost extinct. However, the area that they were harvesting from was already cleared of other plants, making it easier to harvest murnong (also known as yam daisy) exclusively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPascoe2018-117]

Along the northern coast of Australia, [parsnip yams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsnip_yam) were harvested by leaving the bottom part of the yam still stuck in the ground so that it would grow again in the same spot.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGammage2011-118] Aboriginal peoples used slash-and-burn techniques to enrich the nutrients of their soil. However, sheep and cattle later brought over by Europeans would ruin this soil by trampling on it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGammage2011-118] They deliberately exchanged seeds to begin growing plants where they did not naturally occur.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGerritsen2008-119] [Norman Tindale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norman_Tindale) was able to draw an Aboriginal grain belt, detailing the specific areas where plants were once produced.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETindale1974?-120]

In terms of aquaculture, explorer [Thomas Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Mitchell_\(explorer\)) noted large stone fish traps on the Darling River at Brewarrina. Each trap covers a pool, herding fish through a small entrance that would later be shut. Traps were created at different heights to accommodate different water levels during floods and droughts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVinesHope1994-121]

Technology used by Indigenous Australian societies before European contact included weapons, tools, shelters, watercraft, and the [message stick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Message_stick). Weapons included [boomerangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boomerang), spears (sometimes thrown with a [woomera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woomera_\(spear-thrower\))) with stone or fishbone tips, clubs, and (less commonly) axes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Herald''19193-122] The [Stone Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_Age) tools available included knives with ground edges, grinding devices, and eating containers. [Fibrecraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_fibrecraft) was well-developed, and fibre nets, baskets, and bags were used for fishing, hunting, and carrying liquids. Trade networks spanned the continent, and transportation included [canoes](99960.md). Shelters varied regionally, and included [wiltjas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiltja) in the [Atherton Tablelands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atherton_Tablelands), [paperbark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paperbark) and [stringybark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stringybark) sheets and raised platforms in [Arnhem Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnhem_Land), whalebone [huts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hut) in what is now South Australia, stone shelters in what is now western Victoria, and a multi-room pole and bark structure found in [Corranderrk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corranderrk).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEaustralia.gov.au2016-123] A bark tent or lean-to is known as a [humpy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humpy), gunyah, or wurley. Clothing included the [possum-skin cloak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Possum-skin_cloak) in the southeast, [buka cloak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buka_cloak) in the southwest and [riji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riji) (pearl shells) in the northeast.

There is evidence that some Aboriginal populations in northern Australia regularly [traded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makassan_contact_with_Australia) with [Makassan](389464.md) fishermen from Indonesia before the arrival of Europeans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacKnight198669–75-124][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPascoe2015-125]

#### Population

The Indigenous population prior to British settlement has been estimated from 300,000[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_1301.02002-18] to more than 3,000,000.[^cite_note-\:3-19][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGough2011-126][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliam2013-100] Recent archaeological finds suggest that a population of 500,000 to 750,000 could have been sustained, with some ecologists estimating that a population of one million to two million people was possible.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGough2011-126][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHugo2012-127]

The geographic distribution of the pre-contact population is a matter of academic debate. Pardoe argues that the distribution was similar to that of the current Australian population, the majority living in the south-east, centred along the [Murray River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murray_River).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPardoe20061–21-128] However, Evans suggests that the area which is now Queensland was the most densely populated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans2007-129]

In 2011, Ørsted-Jensen proposed the following distribution of the pre-contact population:

| State/territory | Estimates of population share | Pre-contact |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1930 | 1988 |  |  |
| Queensland | 38.2% | 37.9% | 34.2% |
| Western Australia | 19.7% | 20.2% | 22.1% |
| Northern Territory | 15.9% | 12.6% | 17.2% |
| New South Wales | 15.3% | 18.9% | 10.3% |
| Victoria | 4.8% | 5.7% | 5.7% |
| South Australia | 4.8% | 4.0% | 8.6% |
| Tasmania | 1.4% | 0.6% | 2.0% |

#### Torres Strait Island people

The Torres Strait peoples' fishing economy relied on boats, which they built themselves. There is also evidence of the construction of large, complex [buildings on stilts](1879233.md) and [domed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome) structures using [bamboo](39029.md), with [thatched roofs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thatched_roofs), which catered for extended family members living together.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPascoe2018-117]

### British colonisation

#### First contact



A 1770 sketch of two Aboriginal men by the British explorer [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook)'s illustrator Sidney Parkinson

British exploration of the Australian coastline began with the buccaneer [William Dampier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Dampier) in 1688 and 1699. Dampier was impressed neither by the country nor the people of the west Australian coast.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBach1966-131] Almost a century later, the explorer [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook) mapped the east coast of Australia and claimed the territory for Britain in the name of [King George III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_George_III).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Cook,_James''1966-132] Cook was impressed both by the land and the people whom he encountered, writing in his journal: "From what I have said of the Natives of [New Holland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Holland_\(Australia\)) they may appear to some to be the most wretched people upon Earth, but in reality they are far more happier than we Europeans; being wholy unacquainted not only with the superfluous but the necessary conveniencies so much sought after in Europe, they are happy in not knowing the use of them. They live in a Tranquillity which is not disturb'd by the Inequality of Condition".[^cite_note-133]

Nevertheless, Cook also noted in his journal two men at Botany Bay who "seem'd resolved to oppose" his first landing. According to Cook, after one of the men threw a rock, Cook fired a musquet loaded with small shott, which struck him with little effect.[^cite_note-134] Some shott was lodged into one of the men's shields and was taken back to England by Cook, where it remains in the British Museum.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHigginsCollard2020-135]

Cook spent a greater period of time among the Guugu Yimithirr people around modern [Cooktown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooktown) in Queensland, where his ship was nearly wrecked on the Great Barrier Reef. Here relations were generally amicable and Cook recorded words from their language including "kangaroo", though a fight broke out when the British took turtles from the river without sharing them. Peace was restored when an elder presented Cook with a broken-tipped spear as a peace offering – remembered as a first "act of reconciliation". The encounter is commemorated annually by the Guugu Yimithirr to this day.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKimStephen2020-136]

Cook's favourable impression of the East Coast of Australia led directly to the commencement of the British colonisation of Australia, commencing at Sydney in 1788. The [First Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Fleet) of British ships was commanded by Governor [Arthur Phillip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Phillip), who had been instructed to "endeavour by every possible means to open an intercourse with the natives, and to conciliate their affections", and to enjoin his British subjects to "live in amity and kindness with them" so as "to cultivate an acquaintance with them without their having an idea of our great superiority over them".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoyal2017-137]

#### Dates by area



[Wurundjeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wurundjeri) people at the signing of [Batman's Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Batman's_Treaty), 1835

[British colonisation of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_colonisation_of_Australia) began with the arrival of the [First Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Fleet) in [Botany Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botany_Bay), New South Wales, in 1788. Settlements were subsequently established in [Tasmania](29944.md) (1803), [Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_\(Australia\)) (1803), [Queensland](59051.md) (1824), [Western Australia](33613.md) (1826), and the [Colony of South Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_South_Australia) (1836).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBroome201018-138]

The first settlement in the [Northern Territory](21638.md) was built after Captain [Gordon Bremer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gordon_Bremer) took possession of the [Tiwi Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiwi_Islands) of [Bathurst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bathurst_Island_\(Australia\)) and [Melville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melville_Island_\(Australia\)), claiming them for the [colony of New South Wales](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_New_South_Wales), although that settlement failed after a few years,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorris2001245-139] along with a couple of later attempts; permanent settlement was only finally achieved at [Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darwin,_Northern_Territory) in 1869.

Australia was the exception to British imperial colonisation practices, in that no treaty was drawn up setting out terms of agreement between the settlers and native proprietors, as was the case in North America and [New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBroome201018-138] Many of the men on the First Fleet had had military experience among Native American tribes in North America, and tended to attribute to the Aboriginal people alien and misleading systems or concepts like [chieftainship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribal_chief) and [tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribe) with which they had become acquainted in the northern hemisphere.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFoley2007177–179-140]

British administrative control began in the Torres Strait Islands in 1862, with the appointment of John Jardine, police magistrate at [Rockhampton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockhampton), as Government Resident in the Torres Straits. He originally established a small settlement on [Albany Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albany_Island), but on 1 August 1864 he went to Somerset Island.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELack1963132–153-141] English missionaries arrived on [Erub (Darnley Island)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darnley_Island,_Torres_Strait) on 1 July 1871.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETSRA_–_history-142] In 1872 the boundary of Queensland was extended to include [Thursday Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thursday_Island) and some other islands in Torres Strait within 60 miles (97 km) of the Queensland coast, and in 1879 Queensland [annexed](18950961.md) the other islands, which became part of the British [colony of Queensland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Queensland).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELack1963132–153-141]

#### Impact

One immediate consequence was a series of epidemics of European diseases such as [measles](58911.md), [smallpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox) and [tuberculosis](30653.md). In the 19th century, smallpox was the principal cause of Aboriginal deaths, and vaccinations of the "native inhabitants" had begun in earnest by the 1840s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGlynnJenifer2004145–146-21] This smallpox epidemic in 1789 is estimated to have killed up to 90% of the [Darug people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darug_people). The cause of the outbreak is disputed. Some scholars have attributed it to European settlers,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeissMelodie-Jane2013-143][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackwell_Public_Schooln.d.-144] but it is also argued that [Macassan](389464.md) fishermen from [South Sulawesi](389458.md) and nearby islands may have introduced smallpox to Australia before the arrival of Europeans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell2007-145] A third suggestion is that the outbreak was caused by contact with members of the [First Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Fleet).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMear20081–22-146] A fourth theory is that the epidemic was of [chickenpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chickenpox), not smallpox, carried by members of the First Fleet, and to which the Aboriginal people also had no immunity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcIlroy2013-147][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECarmody2013-148][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKociumbas200479ff-149][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''United_Service''20147-150] Moreover, Aboriginal people were infected with sexually transmitted infections, especially syphilis and gonorrhea.

Another consequence of British colonisation was European seizure of land and water resources, with the decimation of kangaroo and other food sources which continued throughout the 19th and early 20th centuries as rural lands were converted for sheep and cattle grazing.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPascoe2007182–185-151] Settlers also participated in the rape and forcible prostitution of Aboriginal women.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnor2016-152]

Some Europeans, for example escaped convicts, lived in Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander communities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaynardHaskins2016-153]

In 1834 there occurred the first recorded use of [Aboriginal trackers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_tracker), who proved very adept at navigating their way through the Australian landscape and finding people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpoonerFirmanYalmambirra2013329-154]

During the 1860s, [Tasmanian Aboriginal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasmanian_Aboriginal) skulls were particularly sought internationally for studies into [craniofacial anthropometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craniofacial_anthropometry). The skeleton of [Truganini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Truganini), a Tasmanian Aboriginal who died in 1876, was exhumed within two years of her death despite her pleas to the contrary by the [Royal Society of Tasmania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Society_of_Tasmania), and later placed on display. Campaigns continue to have Aboriginal body parts returned to Australia for burial; Truganini's body was returned in 1976 and cremated, and her ashes were scattered according to her wishes.

Place names sometimes reveal discrimination, such as Mount Jim Crow in [Rockhampton, Queensland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rockhampton,_Queensland) (now [Mount Baga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Baga)), as well as racist policies, like Brisbane's Boundary Streets, which used to indicate boundaries where Aboriginal people were not allowed to cross during certain times of the day.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECondon2011-155] There is ongoing discussion about changing many of these names.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcMah2016-156][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETerzonRobinson2018-157]



Graph showing the destination of Indigenous wages in Queensland in the 19th and 20th centuries

Throughout most of the 19th and 20th centuries, Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people had their lives under the jurisdiction of various state-based protection laws. These [Acts of Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Parliament) appointed [Protectors of Aborigines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectors_of_Aborigines) and [Aboriginal Protection Boards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_Protection_Board), whose role was to control the lives of Indigenous Australians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENMA:_APA2021-158] Wages were controlled by the Protectors, and Indigenous Australians received less income than their non-Indigenous counterparts in employment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoQ-159][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoQ2016-160]

During this time, many Aboriginal people were victims of slavery by colonists alongside [Pacific Islander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islander) peoples who were kidnapped from their homes, in a practice known as [blackbirding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackbirding). Between 1860 and 1970, under the guise of protectionist policies, people, including children as young as 12, were forced to work on properties where they worked under horrific conditions and most did not receive any wages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESimmonds2015-161] In the [pearling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl_hunting) industry, Aboriginal peoples were bought for about 5 pounds, with pregnant Aboriginal women "prized because their lungs were believed to have greater air capacity".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorff2018b-162] Aboriginal prisoners in the Aboriginal-only prison on [Rottnest Island](216061.md), many of whom were there on trumped up charges, were chained up and forced to work.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurphy2017-163] In 1971, 373 Aboriginal men were found buried in unmarked graves on the island.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMelville2016-164] Up until June 2018, the former prison was being used as holiday accommodation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWynne2018-165]



The successive breeding out of "colour" in the Aboriginal population as part of assimilation policies, demonstrated here in [A. O. Neville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._O._Neville)'s "Australia's coloured minority" book

From 1810, Aboriginal peoples were moved onto mission stations, run by churches and the state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorff2018a-166] After this period of protectionist policies that aimed to segregate and control Aboriginal populations, in 1937 the Commonwealth government agreed to move towards assimilation policies. These policies aimed to integrate Aboriginal persons who were "not of full blood" into the white community in an effort to eliminate the "Aboriginal problem". As part of this, there was an increase in the number of children forcibly removed from their homes and placed with white people, either in institutions or foster homes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Face_the_Facts''2005-167]

#### Frontier wars and massacres

Main articles: [Australian frontier wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_frontier_wars) and [List of massacres of Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_massacres_of_Indigenous_Australians)



New South Wales Mounted Police killing Aboriginal warriors during the [Waterloo Creek massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterloo_Creek_massacre), 1838



Aboriginal prisoners on [Rottnest Island](216061.md), 1883

As part of the colonisation process, there were many conflicts and clashes between colonists and Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people across the continent and islands. In Queensland, the killing of Aboriginal peoples was largely perpetrated by civilian "hunting" parties and the Native Police, armed groups of Aboriginal men who were recruited at gunpoint and led by government officers to eliminate Aboriginal resistance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERichards2014147–161-168] There is evidence that massacres of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples, which began with arrival of British colonists, continued until the 1930s. Researchers at the [University of Newcastle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Newcastle_\(Australia\)) under [Lyndall Ryan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndall_Ryan) have been mapping the massacres.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Newcastle2017b-169] as of 2020 they have mapped almost 500 places where massacres happened, with 12,361 Aboriginal people killed and 204 Colonists killed,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUoN-170] numbering at least 311 massacres over a period of about 140 years. After losing a significant number of their social unit in one blow, the survivors were left very vulnerable – with reduced ability to gather food, reproduce, or fulfill their ceremonial obligations, as well as defend themselves against further attack.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEColonial_frontier_massacres_–_C21CH-171]

Estimating the total number of deaths during the frontier wars is difficult due to lack of records and the fact that many massacres of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander were kept secret.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniversity_of_Newcastle2017b-169] It is often quoted that 20,000 Aboriginal Australians and 2000 colonists died in the frontier wars;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFirst_encounters_and_frontier_conflict2015-172] however, recent research indicates at least 40,000 Aboriginal dead and 2,000 to 2,500 settlers dead.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReynolds2013121–134-173] Other research indicates a minimum of 65,000 Aboriginal peoples may have been killed in Queensland alone.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDaley2014-174]

There have been arguments over whether deaths of Aboriginal peoples, particularly in Tasmania, as well as the forcible removal of children from Aboriginal communities, constitutes [genocide](12441.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarman2018Moses2017RogersBain201683–100-175] There has been a broad range of historical research on the massacres and treatment of Aboriginal peoples, including by Lyndall Ryan at the Centre for 21st Century Humanities,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERyan2021-176] the Frontier Conflict Database,[^cite_note-177] and the Australian Commonwealth government's Human Rights and Equal Opportunity Commission (HREOC) Report of the National Inquiry into the Separation of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Children From their Families.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1997275-178]

According to the analysis of Justice Ronald Wilson in the Human Rights and Equal Opportunity Report Australia's policy of forcible removal was genocidal in nature. Quoting Raphael Lemkin, Wilson defined genocide as "a coordinated plan of different actions aimed at the destruction of the essential foundations of the lives of national groups, with the aim of annihilating the groups themselves."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELemkin194479-179] The objectives of which were "the disintegration of the political and social institutions, of culture, language, national feelings, religion, and the economic existence of national groups, the destruction of personal security, liberty, health, dignity, and even the lives of the individuals belonging to such groups."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELemkin194479-179]

Wilson states that "Genocide can be committed by means other than actual physical extermination. It is committed by the forcible transfer of children, provided the other elements of the crime are established."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1997275-178] He points out that "Genocide is committed even when the destruction has not been carried out. A conspiracy to commit genocide and an attempt at genocide are both crimes which are committed whether or not any actual destruction occurred."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1997275-178] Further, Wilson found that "The debates at the time of the drafting of the Genocide Convention establish clearly that an act or policy is still genocidal when it is motivated by a number of objectives. To constitute an act of genocide the planned extermination of a group need not be solely motivated by animosity or hatred."...and that "reasonable foreseeability...is sufficient to establish the Convention's intent element."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1997275-178] He concluded that "The policy of forcible removal of children from Indigenous Australians to other groups for the purpose of raising them separately from and ignorant of their culture and people could properly be labelled 'genocidal' in breach of binding international law from at least 11 December 1946...The practice continued for almost another quarter of a century."[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilson1997275-178]

There are few memorials in Australia acknowledging the widespread massacres of Aboriginal Peoples, and no memorials describing it as genocide. However, the massacres were often recorded by Australians as place names, for example: [Murdering Gully](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murdering_Gully_massacre) in Newcastle, Murdering Creek at Lake Weyba, Skull Pocket and Skeleton Creek near Cairns, Rifle Creek near Mt Molloy Qld, Skull Lagoon near Mt Carbine Qld, Skull Hole near Winton Qld, Battle Camp Road, Range and Station east of Laura Qld, Slaughterhouse Creek (Waterloo Creek) NSW.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECronshaw2018Bottoms20131–208-180]

Main article: [Stolen Generations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_Generations)

The Stolen Generations were those children of Australian Aboriginal and [Torres Strait Islander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islander) descent who were forcibly removed[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFOCUS_Asia-Pacific1997-181] from their families by the Australian [federal](1222540.md) and [state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_states_and_territories) government agencies and [church missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_\(Christian\)) for the purpose of eradicating Aboriginal culture, under [acts of their respective parliaments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_parliament).[^cite_note-182][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBringing_them_home_education_module-183] The forcible removal of these children occurred in the period between approximately 1871[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarten2002229-184] and 1969,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Museum2004-185][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERead1981-186] although in some places children were still being taken in the 1970s.[^cite_note-187]

### Early 20th century

By 1900, the recorded Indigenous population of Australia had declined to approximately 93,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_1301.02002-18] However, this was only a partial count, as both Aboriginal people and Torres Strait Islanders were poorly covered, with desert Aboriginal peoples not counted at all until the 1930s. During the first half of the twentieth century, many Indigenous Australians worked as [stockmen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockman_\(Australia\)) on [sheep stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheep_station) and [cattle stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle_station) for extremely low wages. The Indigenous population continued to decline, reaching a low of 74,000 in 1933 before numbers began to recover. By 1995, population numbers had reached pre-colonisation levels, and in 2010 there were around 563,000 Indigenous Australians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHugo2012-127]

Although, as [British subjects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_subject), all Indigenous Australians were nominally entitled to vote, generally only those who merged into mainstream society did so. Only Western Australia and Queensland specifically excluded Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people from the electoral rolls. Despite the [Commonwealth Franchise Act 1902](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Franchise_Act_1902), which excluded "Aboriginal natives of Australia, Asia, Africa and Pacific Islands except New Zealand" from voting unless they were on the roll before 1901, South Australia insisted that all voters enfranchised within its borders would remain eligible to vote in the Commonwealth, and Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people continued to be added to their rolls, albeit haphazardly.



Aboriginal women carrying a child wrapped in [pelt cloak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Possum-skin_cloak), [South Australia](26716.md), c. 1860

Despite efforts to bar their enlistment, over 1,000 Indigenous Australians fought for Australia in the First World War.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELondey-188]

1934 saw the first appeal to the [High Court](314201.md) by an Aboriginal Australian, and it succeeded. Dhakiyarr was found to have been wrongly convicted of the murder of a white policeman, for which he had been sentenced to death; the case focused national attention on [Aboriginal rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_rights) issues. Dhakiyarr disappeared upon release.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENAA_Uncommon_Lives2004-189][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustLII1934-190] In 1938, the 150th anniversary of the arrival of the British [First Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Fleet) was marked as a [Day of Mourning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_of_Mourning_\(Australia\)) and Protest at an Aboriginal meeting in Sydney, and has since become marked around Australia as "Invasion Day" or "Survival Day" by Aboriginal protesters and their supporters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKooriweb.org1938-191]

Hundreds of Indigenous Australians served in the Australian armed forces during World War Two – including with the [Torres Strait Light Infantry Battalion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Light_Infantry_Battalion) and The [Northern Territory Special Reconnaissance Unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Territory_Special_Reconnaissance_Unit), which were established to guard [Australia's North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Australia) against the threat of Japanese invasion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAWM-192] However, most were denied pension rights and military allotments, except in Victoria, where each case was judged individually, without a blanket denial of rights accruing from their service.[^cite_note-193]

### Late 20th century



Picture of [Albert Namatjira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Namatjira) at the [Albert Namatjira Gallery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Namatjira_Gallery), [Alice Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_Springs,_Northern_Territory). [Aboriginal art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_art) and artists became increasingly prominent in Australian cultural life during the second half of the 20th century.



Australian tennis player [Evonne Goolagong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evonne_Goolagong)

Further information: [Indigenous land rights in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_land_rights_in_Australia)

The 1960s was a pivotal decade in the assertion of Aboriginal rights and a time of growing collaboration between Aboriginal activists and white Australian activists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENMoA_–_indigenous_rights-194] In 1962, Commonwealth legislation guaranteed Aboriginal people the right to vote in [Commonwealth elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Australia), which had previously been denied to Indigenous people in Queensland and Western Australia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAEC_–_milestones-195] A group of [University of Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Sydney) students organised a bus tour of western and coastal New South Wales towns in 1965 to raise awareness of the state of Aboriginal health and living conditions. This [Freedom Ride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Ride_\(Australia\)) also aimed to highlight the social discrimination faced by Aboriginal people and encourage Aboriginal people themselves to resist discrimination.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENMoA_–_Freedom_Ride-196]

As mentioned above, Indigenous Australians received lower wages than their non-Indigenous counterparts in employment. Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people in Queensland in particular had their income quarantined by the Protector and were allowed a minimal amount of their income.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoQ-159][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoQ2016-160] In 1966, [Vincent Lingiari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vincent_Lingiari) led the famous [Wave Hill walk-off](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_Hill_walk-off) (Gurindji strike) of Indigenous employees of [Wave Hill Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_Hill_Station) in protest against poor pay and conditions[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENMoA_–_walk-off-197] (later the subject of the [Paul Kelly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Kelly_\(Australian_musician\)) and [Kev Carmody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kev_Carmody) song "[From Little Things Big Things Grow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/From_Little_Things_Big_Things_Grow)").[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelly2010-198] Since 1999, the [Queensland Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queensland_Government), under pressure from the [Queensland Council of Unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queensland_Council_of_Unions), has established a number of schemes to give any earned income not received at the time back to Indigenous Australians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoQ-159][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoQ2016-160]

The landmark [1967 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Australian_referendum_\(Aboriginals\)) called by Prime Minister [Harold Holt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Holt) allowed the [Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_federal_government) to make laws with respect to Aboriginal people by modifying [section 51(xxvi)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_51\(xxvi\)_of_the_Australian_Constitution) of the Constitution, and for Aboriginal people to be included when the country does a count to determine electoral representation by repealing [section 127](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_127_of_the_Australian_Constitution). The referendum passed with 90.77% voter support.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENMoA1968-199]

In the controversial 1971 [Gove land rights case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gove_land_rights_case), Justice Blackburn ruled that Australia had been *[terra nullius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terra_nullius)* before British settlement, and that no concept of [native title existed in Australian law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_title_in_Australia). Following the 1973 [Woodward commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_Land_Rights_Commission), in 1975 the federal government under [Gough Whitlam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gough_Whitlam) drafted the Aboriginal Land Rights Bill. This was enacted the following year under the [Fraser government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraser_government) as the *[Aboriginal Land Rights (Northern Territory) Act 1976](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_Land_Rights_\(Northern_Territory\)_Act_1976)*, which recognised Aboriginal Australians' system of land rights in the Northern Territory, and established the basis upon which Aboriginal people in the NT could claim rights to land based on [traditional occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_owner).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFogartyDwyer2012-200][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoAD-201][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECentral_Land_Council-202][^cite_note-FOOTNOTELawfordZillman2016-203]

In 1985, the Australian government returned ownership of [Uluru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uluru) (Ayers Rock) to the [Pitjantjatjara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitjantjatjara_people) Aboriginal people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAIATSIS:_handback2015-204] In 1992, the [High Court of Australia](314201.md) reversed Justice Blackburn's ruling and handed down its decision in the [Mabo Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mabo_v_Queensland_\(No_2\)_\(1992\)), declaring the previous legal concept of *terra nullius* to be invalid and confirming the existence of [native title in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_title_in_Australia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together:_Mabo_and_Native_title-205][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAIATSIS:_Land_Rights2015-206]

Indigenous Australians began to serve in parliaments from the late 1960s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGobbett2017-207] In 1971, [Neville Bonner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neville_Bonner) joined the [Australian Senate](165705.md) as a Senator for Queensland for the [Liberal Party](18453.md), becoming the first Indigenous Australian in the Federal Parliament. A year later, the [Aboriginal Tent Embassy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_Tent_Embassy) was established on the steps of [Parliament House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Parliament_House,_Canberra) in [Canberra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canberra). In 1976, Sir [Douglas Nicholls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Nicholls) was appointed as the 28th Governor of South Australia, the first Aboriginal person appointed to vice-regal office.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECivics_and_Citizenship_Education2005-208] In the [general election of 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Australian_federal_election), [Ken Wyatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Wyatt) of the [Liberal Party](18453.md) became the first Indigenous Australian elected to the [Australian House of Representatives](61565.md). In the [general election of 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Australian_federal_election), [Linda Burney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Burney) of the [Australian Labor Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party) became the second Indigenous Australian, and the first Indigenous Australian woman, elected to the [Australian House of Representatives](61565.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC2016b-209] She was immediately appointed [Shadow Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadow_Cabinet_of_Australia) for Human Services.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENorman2016-210]

In sport [Evonne Goolagong Cawley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evonne_Goolagong_Cawley) became the world number-one ranked tennis player in 1971 and won 14 Grand Slam titles during her career. In 1973 [Arthur Beetson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Beetson) became the first Indigenous Australian to captain his country in any sport when he first led the Australian National Rugby League team, [the Kangaroos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_national_rugby_league_team).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArthur_Beetson_OAM-211] In 1982, [Mark Ella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Ella) became captain of the [Australia national rugby union team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_national_rugby_union_team).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERugby_Hall_of_Fame2007-212] In 2000, Aboriginal sprinter [Cathy Freeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathy_Freeman) lit the [Olympic flame](74217.md) at the opening ceremony of the [2000 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Summer_Olympics) in Sydney, and went on to win the [400 metres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/400_metres) at the Games. In 2019, tennis player [Ashleigh Barty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashleigh_Barty) was ranked world number one.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC_News2019-213]

In 1984, [a group of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pintupi_Nine) [Pintupi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pintupi) people who were living a traditional [hunter-gatherer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer) desert-dwelling life were tracked down in the [Gibson Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibson_Desert) in Western Australia and brought in to a settlement. They are believed to have been the last [uncontacted people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncontacted_peoples) in Australia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEThorley2006-214][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENMoA_–_contact-215]

During this period, the federal government enacted a number of significant, but controversial, policy initiatives in relation to Indigenous Australians. A representative body, the [Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_and_Torres_Strait_Islander_Commission) (ATSIC), was set up in 1990.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPratt2003-216]

#### Reconciliation

Main article: [Reconciliation in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconciliation_in_Australia)

Reconciliation between non-Indigenous and Indigenous Australians became a significant issue in Australian politics in the late 20th century. In 1991, the [Council for Aboriginal Reconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_for_Aboriginal_Reconciliation) was established by the federal government to facilitate reconciliation. In 1998, a Constitutional Convention which selected a Republican model for a referendum included just six Indigenous participants, leading Monarchist delegate [Neville Bonner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neville_Bonner) to end his contribution to the convention with his Jagera tribal "Sorry Chant" in sadness at the low number of Indigenous representatives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcKenna2000-217]

An inquiry into the [Stolen Generations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_Generations) was launched in 1995 by the [Keating government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keating_government), and the final report delivered in 1997 – the *Bringing Them Home* report – estimated that around 10% to 33% of all Aboriginal children had been separated from their families for the duration of the policies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together:_Stolen_Generations-218] The succeeding [Howard government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_government) largely ignored the recommendations provided by the report, one of which was a formal apology to Aboriginal Australians for the Stolen Generations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together:_Stolen_Generations-218]

The republican model, as well as a proposal for a new Constitutional preamble which would have included the "honouring" of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people, was put to [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Australian_republic_referendum) but did not succeed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcKenna2000-217] In 1999, the Australian Parliament passed a [Motion of Reconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_of_Reconciliation) drafted by Prime Minister [John Howard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Howard) in consultation with Aboriginal Senator [Aden Ridgeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aden_Ridgeway) naming mistreatment of Indigenous Australians as the most "blemished chapter in our national history", although Howard refused to offer any formal apology.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrennan2008-219]

On 13 February 2008, Prime Minister [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) issued a formal [apology to Australia's Indigenous peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apology_to_Australia's_Indigenous_peoples), on behalf of the federal government of Australia, for the suffering caused by the Stolen Generations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWelch2008-220]

### 21st century

In 2001, the Federal Government dedicated [Reconciliation Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconciliation_Place) in Canberra. On 13 February 2008, Prime Minister [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) reversed Howard's decision and issued a [public apology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apology_to_Australia's_Indigenous_peoples) to members of the [Stolen Generations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_Generations) on behalf of the Australian Government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoA2012-221]

ATSIC was abolished by the Australian Government in 2004 amidst allegations of corruption.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPratt2003-216]

#### Emergency Response/Stronger Futures

The [Northern Territory National Emergency Response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Territory_National_Emergency_Response) (also known as the Intervention) was launched in 2007 by the government of Prime Minister [John Howard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Howard), in response to the *[Little Children are Sacred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Children_are_Sacred)* report into allegations of [child abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_abuse) among Aboriginal communities in the NT. The government banned alcohol in prescribed communities in the Territory; quarantined a percentage of [welfare payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_security_in_Australia) for essential goods purchasing; dispatched additional police and medical personnel to the region; and suspended the permit system for access to Aboriginal communities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGibson2007-222] In addition to these measures, the [army](2795.md) were released into communities[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCallumWaller201233–34-223] and there were increased police powers, which were later further increased with the so-called "paperless arrests" legislation.[^cite_note-224]

In 2010, United Nations Special Rapporteur James Anaya found the Emergency Response to be racially discriminatory, and said that aspects of it represented a limitation on "individual autonomy".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnaya2010-225][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKirk2010-226] These findings were criticised by the government's Indigenous Affairs Minister [Jenny Macklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jenny_Macklin), the Opposition and Indigenous leaders like [Warren Mundine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Mundine) and [Bess Price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bess_Price).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHawley2009-227][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC2011-228]

In 2011, the Australian government enacted legislation to implement the [Stronger Futures policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stronger_Futures_policy), which is intended to address key issues that exist within Aboriginal communities of the Northern Territory such as unemployment, school attendance and enrolment, [alcohol abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_abuse), community safety and child protection, food security and housing and land reforms. The policy has been criticised by organisations such as [Amnesty International](18947898.md) and other groups, including on the basis that it maintains "racially-discriminatory" elements of the *Emergency Response Act* and continues control by the federal government over "Aboriginal people and their lands".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''Stand_for_Freedom''2012-229]

#### Constitutional change proposed

In 2010, the federal government appointed a panel comprising Indigenous leaders, other legal experts and some members of parliament (including Ken Wyatt) to provide advice on how best to recognise Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples in the [federal Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Constitution). The panel's recommendations, reported to the federal government in January 2012,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECoA2012-221] included deletion of provisions of the Constitution referencing race ([Section 25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_25_of_the_Constitution_of_Australia) and [Section 51(xxvi)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_51\(xxvi\)_of_the_Australian_Constitution)), and new provisions on meaningful recognition and further protection from discrimination.[^cite_note-230] Subsequently, a [proposed referendum on Constitutional recognition of Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proposed_2013_Australian_constitutional_referendum) was abandoned in 2013.

The *[Uluru Statement from the Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uluru_Statement_from_the_Heart)*[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUluru_Statement_from_the_Heart-231] was released 26 May 2017 by delegates to an Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Referendum Convention, held near [Uluru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uluru) in Central Australia. The statement calls for a "First Nations Voice" in the Australian Constitution and a "Makarrata Commission" to supervise a process of "agreement-making" and "truth-telling" between government and Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUluru_Statement:_a_quick_guide-232] The statement references the [1967 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_referendum,_1967_\(Aboriginals\)) which brought about changes to the Constitution to include Indigenous Australians.

In 2023, the federal government held the [Indigenous Voice referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Australian_Indigenous_Voice_referendum) to recognise Indigenous peoples in the constistution, and establish an advisory body to the government to represent Indigenous interests. The proposal was rejected by Australian voters, having been opposed by the [Liberal–National Coalition](1051313.md).

## Population

The Australian Bureau of Statistics estimates that in 2021 there were 983,700 Indigenous Australians, of whom 901,700 were Aboriginal, 39,540 Torres Strait Islander, and 39,540 identifying as both Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander.[^cite_note-auto1-1]
