The Indigenous population from 1971 to 2021 are given below. These are based on initial census counts and have not been adjusted for undercounting and other factors.[^cite_note-changes-233]

| Census | Population | Percentage |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1971 | 115,953 | 0.9% |
| 1976 | 160,915 | 1.2% |
| 1981 | 159,897 | 1.1% |
| 1986 | 206,104 | 1.5% |
| 1991 | 265,378 | 1.6% |
| 1996 | 352,970 | 2.0% |
| 2006 | 455,028 | 2.2% |
| 2011 | 548,368 | 2.5% |
| [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Australian_census) | 649,171 | 2.7% |
| [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Australian_census) | 812,728 | 3.2% |

### Definition

See also: [Australian Aboriginal identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_identity)

&#x20;Over time Australia has used various means to determine membership of ethnic groups such as [lineage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lineage_\(genetic\)), [blood quantum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_quantum_laws), birth and [self-determination](29269.md). From 1869 until well into the 1970s, children under 12 years of age with 25% or less Aboriginal blood were considered "white" and were often [removed from their families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_generations) by the Australian [Federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_government) and [State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_states_and_territories) government agencies and [church missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_\(Christian\)), under [acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_parliament) of their respective parliaments in order that they would have "a reasonable chance of absorption into the white community to which they rightly belong".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBleakley192917-236] Grey areas in determination of ethnicity led to people of mixed ancestry being caught in the middle of divisive policies which often led to absurd situations:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpencer200633–34-237]

> In 1935, an Australian of part Indigenous descent left his home on a reserve to visit a nearby hotel where he was ejected for being Aboriginal. He returned home but was refused entry to the reserve because he was not Aboriginal. He attempted to remove his children from the reserve but was told he could not because they were Aboriginal. He then walked to the next town where he was arrested for being an Aboriginal vagrant and sent to the reserve there. During [World War II](32927.md) he tried to enlist but was rejected because he was an Aborigine so he moved to another state where he enlisted as a non-Aborigine. After the end of the war he applied for a passport but was rejected as he was an Aborigine, he obtained an exemption under the Aborigines Protection Act but was now told he could no longer visit his relatives as he was not an Aborigine. He was later told he could not join the [Returned Servicemens Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Returned_and_Services_League_of_Australia) because he was an Aborigine.

In 1983 the [High Court of Australia](314201.md) (in the *[Commonwealth v Tasmania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_v_Tasmania)* or "*Tasmanian dam(s) case*")[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustLII1983-238] defined an Aboriginal or Torres Strait Islander as "a person of Aboriginal or Torres Strait Islander descent who identifies as an Aboriginal or Torres Strait Islander and is accepted as such by the community in which he or she lives". The ruling was a three-part definition comprising descent, self-identification and community identification. The first part – descent – was genetic descent and unambiguous, but led to cases where a lack of records to prove ancestry excluded some. Self- and community identification were more problematic as they meant that an Indigenous person separated from his or her community due to a family dispute could no longer identify as Aboriginal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEALRC2010-239][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnell1998-240]

As a result, there arose court cases throughout the 1990s where excluded people demanded that their Aboriginality be recognised. As a result, lower courts refined the High Court test when subsequently applying it. In 1995, Justice Drummond in the [Federal Court](4688076.md) held in *Gibbs v Capewell* "...either genuine self-identification as Aboriginal alone or Aboriginal communal recognition as such by itself may suffice, according to the circumstances." This contributed to an increase of 31% in the number of people identifying as Indigenous Australians in the 1996 census when compared to the 1991 census.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreg_Gardiner2002-241] In 1998 [Justice Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Merkel) held in *Shaw v Wolf* that Aboriginal descent is "technical" rather than "real" – thereby eliminating a genetic requirement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConnell1998-240] This decision established that anyone can classify him or herself legally as an Aboriginal, provided he or she is accepted as such by his or her community.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEALRC2010-239]

### Demographics

Main article: [Demography of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demography_of_Australia)

#### Inclusion in the national census



Aboriginal boys and men in front of a bush shelter, [Groote Eylandt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groote_Eylandt), c. 1933

Indigenous Australians have been counted in every census albeit only approximately and using inconsistent definitions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArcioni2012300-242][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoss199910-243] Section 127 of the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Australia), which was repealed in 1967, had excluded "aboriginal natives" from being counted in the overall population statistics for each state and territory and nationally with the [Attorney-General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney-General_for_Australia) providing a legal advice that a person was an "aboriginal native" if they were a "full-blood aboriginal".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEArcioni2012296,_301-244][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoss19996-245] As a consequence of section 127, Indigenous Australians in remote areas uninhabited by non-Indigenous Australians were not counted prior to 1967 in censuses and sometimes estimated.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoss19996-245]

Post 1967, Torres Strait Islanders were considered a separate Indigenous people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoss19995-246] Prior to 1947, Torres Strait Islanders were considered to be Aboriginal in censuses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoss19995-246] In the 1947 census, Torres Strait Islanders were considered to be [Polynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesians) and in the 1954 and 1961 censuses were considered to be [Pacific Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islander).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoss19995-246] In the 1966 census, Torres Strait Islanders were considered to be Aboriginal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoss19995-246]

A "Commonwealth working definition" for Indigenous Australians was developed from 1968 and endorsed by [Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Australia) in 1978 which contains elements of descent, [self-identification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_identity) and community recognition in contrast to the earlier preponderance of Aboriginal blood definition.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERoss19993–4-247][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGardiner-Garden2000-248]

As there is no formal procedure for any community to record acceptance, the primary method of determining Indigenous population is from self-identification on census forms. The [Australian Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Census) includes counts based on questions relating to individuals' self-identification as Aboriginal, Torres Strait Islander, or of both origins.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS2016-249] Owing to various difficulties which lead to under-counting, the [Australian Bureau of Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Bureau_of_Statistics) (ABS) follows a set method to estimate total numbers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_tech_note2018-250]

#### Distribution and growth

See also: [Australian Aboriginal identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_identity)

The 2006 Australian census showed growth in the Indigenous population (recorded as 517,000) at twice the rate of overall population growth since 1996, when the Indigenous population stood at 283,000. In the [2011 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_Australian_Census), there was a 20% rise in people who identify as Aboriginal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESBS2012-251] In the [2016 census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Australian_census), there was another 18.4% rise on the 2011 figure. 590,056 respondents identified themselves as Aboriginal, 32,345 [Torres Strait Islander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islander), and a further 26,767 both Aboriginal and [Torres Strait Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islanders).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_Census2017-252] In the [2021 Australian census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Australian_census), 812,000 people identified as Aboriginal or Torres Strait Islander, representing 3.2% of the population. This was an increase from 2.8% in 2016 (i.e. about 25% increase[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelsey-Sugg2022-253]), and 2.5% in 2011.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS:_population_summary2022-254] However, the net undercount of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people was 17.4%,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS:_Census_over/undercount2021-255] and the estimated Indigenous population is around 952,000 to 1,000,000, or just under 4 per cent of the total population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKelsey-Sugg2022-253]

Growth to 2016 was mainly in major cities and along the eastern coast of Australia. In 2018 the ABS published a report exploring the reasons for these findings, with some of the factors behind the increase being higher fertility rates of Indigenous women; people entering the population through migration; variation in census coverage and response rates; and people changing how they self-identify between census years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_increase2018-256] Another factor might be the children of mixed marriages: the proportion of Aboriginal adults married (*de facto* or *[de jure](43594.md)*) to non-Aboriginal spouses increased to 78.2% in the 2016 census,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_2077.02018-257] (up from 74% in 2011,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_2077.0-258] 64% in 1996, 51% in 1991 and 46% in 1986); it was reported in 2002 that up to 88% of the offspring of mixed marriages subsequently self-identify as Indigenous Australians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreg_Gardiner2002-241]

In the 2016, over 33% of the Indigenous population lived in major cities, compared with about 75% of the non-Indigenous population, with a further 24% in "inner regional" areas (compared with 18%), 20% in "outer regional" (8%), while nearly 18% lived in "remote" or "very remote" areas (2%).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_3238.02018-259] (Ten years earlier, 31% were living in major cities and 24% in remote areas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_–_population2006-260])

## Languages

As of 2021, 84% of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islanders spoke only English at home. Nine percent spoke an Australian Indigenous language at home.[^cite_note-261]

### Aboriginal languages

Main articles: [Australian Aboriginal languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_languages), [Australian Aboriginal English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_English), and [Australian Aboriginal sign languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_sign_languages)

Humans arrived in Australia 50,000 to 65,000 years ago[^cite_note-262][^cite_note-263] but it is possible that the ancestor language of existing Indigenous languages is as recent as 12,000 years old.[^cite_note-\:4-264] Over 250 [Australian Aboriginal languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_languages) are thought to have existed at the time of first European contact.[^cite_note-\:92-265] The National Indigenous Languages Survey (NILS) for 2018-19 found that more than 120 Indigenous language varieties were in use or being revived, although 70 of those in use are endangered.[^cite_note-\:12-266] The 2021 census found that 167 Indigenous languages were spoken at home by 76,978 Indigenous Australians.[^cite_note-\:13-267] NILS and the Australian Bureau of Statistics use different classifications for Indigenous Australian languages.[^cite_note-\:16-268]

According to the 2021 census, the classifiable Aboriginal and Torres Strait Island languages with the most speakers are Yumplatok (Torres Strait Creole) (7,596 speakers), Kriol (7,403), [Djambarrpuyngu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dhuwal_language) (3,839), [Pitjantjatjara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pitjantjatjara_dialect) (3,399), [Warlpiri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warlpiri_language) (2,592), [Murrinh Patha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murrinh-patha_language) (2,063) and [Tiwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiwi_language) (2,053). There were also over 10,000 people who spoke an Indigenous language which could not be further defined or classified.[^cite_note-\:17-269]

Several extinct Indigenous languages are being reconstructed. For example, the last fluent speaker of the [Ngarrindjeri language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngarrindjeri_language) died in the late 1960s; using recordings and written records as a guide, a Ngarrindjeri dictionary was published in 2009,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEUniSA2008-270] and the Ngarrindjeri language is today spoken in complete sentences.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS2012-271]

Aboriginal languages are divided into two main groups: the [Pama–Nyungan languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pama%E2%80%93Nyungan_languages) which is spoken across about 90% of the continent, and the non-Pama–Nyungan. Recent research suggests that an ancestor language to all Aboriginal languages was spoken 12,000 years ago and spread across the continent in the past 6,000 years.[^cite_note-\:4-264][^cite_note-272]

### Torres Strait Island languages

Main article: [Torres Strait Island languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Island_languages)

Three languages are spoken on the islands of the [Torres Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait), within Australian territory, by the Melanesian inhabitants of the area: Yumplatok (Torres Strait Creole) (7,596 speakers used the language at home in 2021), [Kalaw Lagaw Ya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalaw_Lagaw_Ya) (875 speakers) and [Meriam Mir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meriam_language) (256 speakers).[^cite_note-\:17-269] Meriam Mir is a [Papuan language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papuan_languages), while Kalaw Lagaw Ya is an Australian language.

### Creoles

A number of English-based [creoles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creole_language) have arisen in Australia after European contact, of which Kriol and Yumplatok (Torres Strait Creole) are among the strongest and fastest-growing Indigenous languages. Kriol is spoken in the [Northern Territory](21638.md) and [Western Australia](33613.md), and Torres Strait Creole in Queensland and south-west Papua. It is estimated that there are 20,000 to 30,000 speakers of Indigenous creole languages.[^cite_note-273]

### Tasmanian languages

Main article: [Tasmanian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasmanian_languages)

Before British colonisation, there were perhaps five to sixteen languages on Tasmania,[^cite_note-274] possibly related to one another in four [language families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_family).[^cite_note-Bowern-275] The last speaker of a traditional Tasmanian language, [Fanny Cochrane Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fanny_Cochrane_Smith), died in 1905.[^cite_note-276] [Palawa kani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palawa_kani) is an in-progress constructed language, built from a composite of surviving words from various Tasmanian Aboriginal languages.[^cite_note-277]

### Indigenous sign languages

Main article: [Australian Aboriginal sign languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_sign_languages)

Traditional Indigenous languages often incorporated sign systems to aid communication with the hearing impaired, to complement verbal communication, and to replace verbal communication when the spoken language was forbidden for cultural reasons. Many of these sign systems are still in use.[^cite_note-278]

### Cross-cultural communications

Cross-cultural miscommunication can sometimes occur between Indigenous and non-Indigenous peoples. According to Michael Walsh and [Ghil'ad Zuckermann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghil'ad_Zuckermann), Western conversational interaction is typically "[dyadic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dyad_\(sociology\))", between two particular people, where eye contact is important and the speaker controls the interaction; and "contained" in a relatively short, defined time frame. However, traditional Aboriginal conversational interaction is "communal", broadcast to many people, eye contact is not important, the listener controls the interaction; and "continuous", spread over a longer, indefinite time frame.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuckermann;_et_al.201512-279][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalsh19977–9-280]

## Belief systems



Depiction of a [corroboree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corroboree) by 19th century Indigenous activist [William Barak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Barak)

### Traditional beliefs

#### Aboriginal

Further information: [Australian Aboriginal religion and mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_religion_and_mythology)

In Aboriginal communities knowledge and decision making is shared between tribal elders. Travellers had to seek elder recognition and acknowledge local Elders – this is increasingly practiced in public events in Australia. Within Aboriginal belief systems, a formative epoch known as "[the Dreaming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dreaming)" or "the Dreamtime" stretches back into the distant past when the creator ancestors known as the First Peoples travelled across the land, and naming as they went. Indigenous Australia's [oral tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_tradition) and religious values are based upon reverence for the land and a belief in this Dreamtime.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAndrews2004424-281] The Dreaming is at once both the ancient time of creation and the present-day reality of Dreaming. Different language and cultural groups each had their own belief structures; these cultures overlapped to a greater or lesser extent, and evolved over time. Major [ancestral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancestral) spirits include the [Rainbow Serpent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rainbow_Serpent), [Baiame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baiame), [Dirawong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirawong) and [Bunjil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bunjil). Knowledge contained in the Dreaming has been passed down through different stories, [songlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songline), dances and ceremonies, and even today provides a framework for ongoing relationships, [kinship responsibilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_kinship) and looking after country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWVA_–_8_interesting_facts-282]

Traditional healers (known as [Ngangkari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngangkari) in the [Western desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Desert_cultural_bloc) areas of Central Australia) were highly respected men and women who not only acted as healers or doctors, but were generally also custodians of important Dreaming stories.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETraditional_Healers201315–19-283]

#### Torres Strait Islander

Further information: [Torres Strait Islanders § Religion and beliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islanders#Religion_and_beliefs)

Torres Strait Islander people have their own traditional belief systems. Stories of the Tagai represent Torres Strait Islanders as sea people, with a connection to the stars, as well as a system of order in which everything has its place in the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWVA_–_8_interesting_facts-282][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEQCAA2018-284] Some Torres Strait Islander people share beliefs similar to the Aboriginal peoples' Dreaming and "Everywhen" concepts, passed down in [oral history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_history).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE'The_Dreaming'-285]

### After colonisation

Further information: [Christianity in Australia § Indigenous Australians and Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Australia#Indigenous_Australians_and_Christianity)

Christianity and [European culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_culture) have had a significant impact on Indigenous Australians, their religion and their culture. As in many [colonial](7299.md) situations, the churches both facilitated the loss of Indigenous culture and religion and also facilitated its maintenance.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMundine2005-286] In some cases, such as at [Hermannsburg, Northern Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermannsburg,_Northern_Territory) and [Piltawodli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piltawodli) in Adelaide, the work of missionaries laid the foundations for later [language revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_revival). The German missionaries [Christian Teichelmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Teichelmann) and [Schürmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clamor_Sch%C3%BCrmann) went to [Adelaide](1148.md) and taught the local [Kaurna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaurna) people only in [their own language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaurna_language) and created textbooks in the language.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELockwood201765–81-287][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmeryn.d.-288] However, some [missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionaries) taught only in English, and some [Christian missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_mission) were involved in the placement of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander children after they were removed from their parents upon orders of the government, and are therefore implicated in the [Stolen Generations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_Generations).

#### Aboriginal peoples

The involvement of Christians in Aboriginal affairs has evolved significantly since 1788.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMundine2005-286] The Churches became involved in mission work among Aboriginal peoples in the 19th century as Europeans came to control much of the continent, and the majority of the population was eventually converted. Colonial clergy such as Sydney's first Catholic archbishop, [John Bede Polding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bede_Polding), strongly advocated for Aboriginal rights and dignity.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Sullivann.d.-289] Around the year 2000, many churches and church organisations officially apologised for past failures to adequately respect Indigenous cultures and address the injustices of the dispossession of Indigenous people.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMundine2005-286][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnglican_Church2005-290]

A small minority of Aboriginal people are [followers of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Australia#Aboriginal_Muslims) as a result of intermarriage with ["Afghan" camel drivers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afghan_cameleers_in_Australia) brought to Australia in the late 19th and early 20th century to help explore and open up the interior.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMercer2003-291]

#### Torres Strait Islander peoples

From the 1870s, [Christianity](5211.md) spread throughout the Torres Strait Islands, and it remains strong today among Torres Strait Islander people everywhere. The [London Missionary Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/London_Missionary_Society) mission led by Rev. Samuel Macfarlane arrived on [Erub (Darnley Island)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darnley_Island,_Torres_Strait) on 1 July 1871, establishing its first base in the region there. The Islanders refer to this as "The Coming of the Light", or "Coming of Light"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorff2019b-292] and all Island communities celebrate the occasion annually on 1 July.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETSRA_–_history-142] However the coming of Christianity did not spell the end of the people's traditional beliefs; their culture informed their understanding of the new religion, as [God as preached in Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Christianity) was welcomed and the new religion was integrated into every aspect of their everyday lives.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorff2019b-292]

### Recent census figures

In the 2016 Census, Australia's Indigenous and non-Indigenous population were broadly similar with 54% (vs 55%) reporting a [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Australia) affiliation, while less than 2% reported traditional beliefs as their religion, and 36% reported no religion. The proportion of Indigenous people who reported no religion has increased gradually since 2001, standing at 36% in 2016. According to "Table 8: Religious Affiliation by Indigenous Status", 347,572 Indigenous people (out of the total 649,171 in Australia) declared an affiliation to some form of Christianity, with a higher proportion of Torres Strait Islander than Aboriginal people in this number. 7,773 reported traditional beliefs; 1,511 Islam; other religions numbered less than 1,000 each. However the question is optional; 48,670 did not respond, and in addition, nearly 4,000 were reported as "inadequately described".[^cite_note-293] (In the 2006 census, 73% of the Indigenous population reported an affiliation with a Christian denomination, 24% reported no religious affiliation and 1% reported affiliation with an Australian Aboriginal traditional religion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_4713.02010a-294])

## Culture

Main article: [Australian Aboriginal culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_culture)

See also: [Indigenous Australians § Blak culture](#Blak_culture)

### Art



[Gwion Gwion rock paintings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwion_Gwion_rock_paintings) in the [Kimberley region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimberley_\(Western_Australia\)) of Western Australia



[Arnhem Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnhem_Land) artist Glen Namundja painting at [Injalak Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Injalak_Arts)



*[Aboriginal Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_Memorial)*, [National Gallery of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Australia)

Main article: [Indigenous Australian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Australian_art)

Further information: [Torres Strait Islanders § Art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islanders#Art)

Australia has a tradition of Aboriginal art which is thousands of years old, the best known forms being Australian rock art and [bark painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bark_painting). Evidence of Aboriginal art can be traced back at least 30,000 years,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECulture_Portal2007-295] with examples of ancient rock art throughout the continent. Some of these are in national parks such as those of the [UNESCO](21786641.md) listed sites at [Uluru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uluru) and [Kakadu National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kakadu_National_Park) in the [Northern Territory](21638.md), but examples can also within protected parks in urban areas such as at [Ku-ring-gai Chase National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ku-ring-gai_Chase_National_Park) in Sydney.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParks_Australia_(1)-296][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEParks_Australia_(2)-297][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENSW_NPWS-298] The [Sydney rock engravings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_rock_engravings) are between 5000 and 200 years old. [Murujuga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murujuga), in north-western [Western Australia](33613.md) was heritage-listed in 2007[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC_–_Pilbara_Rock_Art-299] and inscribed as a [World Heritage Site](44940.md) in July 2025.[^cite_note-300][^cite_note-readfearn2025-301]

In terms of age and abundance, [cave art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cave_art) in Australia is comparable to that of [Lascaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lascaux) and [Altamira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cave_of_Altamira) ([Upper Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Paleolithic) sites in Europe),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Museum2011-302] and Aboriginal art is believed to be the oldest continuing tradition of art in the world.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENGoV-303] There are three major regional styles: the geometric style found in Central Australia, Tasmania, the Kimberley and Victoria, known for its concentric circles, arcs and dots; the simple figurative style found in [Queensland](59051.md); and the complex figurative style found in [Arnhem Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnhem_Land) and [the Kimberley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Kimberley). These designs generally carry significance linked to the spirituality of the [Dreamtime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dreamtime).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECulture_Portal2007-295] Paintings were usually created in earthy colours, from paint made from [ochre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ochre). Such ochres were also used to paint their bodies for ceremonial purposes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERurenga2017-304][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAboriginal_Art_Online2008-305]

Several styles of Aboriginal art have developed in modern times, including the [watercolour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watercolour) paintings of the [Hermannsburg School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermannsburg_School) and the acrylic [Papunya Tula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papunya_Tula) "dot art" movement. Some notable Aboriginal artists include [William Barak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Barak) (c. 1824–1903) and [Albert Namatjira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Namatjira) (1902–1959).

Since the 1970s, Indigenous artists have employed the use of acrylic paints – with styles such as that of the [Western Desert Art Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Desert_Art_Movement) becoming globally renowned 20th-century art movements.

The [National Gallery of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_of_Australia) exhibits a great many Indigenous art works, including those of the [Torres Strait Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islands) who are known for their traditional sculpture and headgear.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENGA_–_ATSI-306]

Aboriginal art has influenced many non-Indigenous artists, such as [Margaret Preston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Preston) (1875–1963) and [Elizabeth Durack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_Durack) (1915–2000).

### Music, dance and ceremony

Main articles: [Indigenous music of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_music_of_Australia) and [Indigenous dance of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_dance_of_Australia)



[Didgeridoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Didgeridoo) player Ŋalkan Munuŋgurr performing with [East Journey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Journey)

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGraves2017-307]



Aboriginal dancers in 1981

Music and dance have formed an integral part of the [social](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society), [cultural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture) and [ceremonial observances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_culture#Ceremonies_and_sacred_objects) of people through the millennia of the individual and collective histories of Australian Indigenous peoples to the present day.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELPP2001-308][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFiona2007-309][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENewton199093–101-310][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunbar-HallGibson200045–73-311] Around 1950, the first research into Aboriginal music was undertaken by the anthropologist [A. P. Elkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._P._Elkin), who recorded Aboriginal music in [Arnhem Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnhem_Land).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELehmann1997-312]

The various Aboriginal peoples developed unique musical instruments and styles. The [didgeridoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Didgeridoo), which is widely thought to be a stereotypical instrument of Aboriginal people, was traditionally played by Aboriginal men of the eastern [Kimberley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimberley_\(Western_Australia\)) region and [Arnhem Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnhem_Land) (such as the Yolngu).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAboriginal_Arts-313] [Bullroarers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bullroarer) and [clapsticks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clapsticks) were used across Australia. [Songlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songline) relate to the [Dreamtime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dreamtime) in Aboriginal culture, overlapping with [oral lore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_lore).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECommon_Ground:_Songlines-314] [Corroboree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corroboree) is a generic word to explain different genres of performance, embracing songs, dances, rallies and meetings of various kinds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESweeney2008-315]

Indigenous musicians have been prominent in various contemporary styles of music, including creating a [sub-genre of rock music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_rock#Australia) as well as participating in [pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pop_music) and other mainstream styles. [Hip hop music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_hop_music) is helping preserve some Indigenous languages.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Age''2006-316]

The [Aboriginal Centre for the Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_Centre_for_the_Performing_Arts) in [Brisbane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brisbane) teaches acting, music and dance, and the [Bangarra Dance Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangarra_Dance_Theatre) is an acclaimed contemporary dance company.

For [Torres Strait Islander people, singing and dancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islanders#Music_and_dance) is their "literature" – "the most important aspect of Torres Strait lifestyle. The Torres Strait Islanders preserve and present their oral history through songs and dances;... the dances act as illustrative material and, of course, the dancer himself is the storyteller" (Ephraim Bani, 1979).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWiltshire2017-317]

### Literature

Main article: [Indigenous Australian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Australian_literature)



[David Unaipon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Unaipon), the first Aboriginal published author



Aboriginal lawyer, activist and essayist [Noel Pearson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noel_Pearson)

There was no written form of the many languages spoken by Indigenous peoples before colonisation. A letter to Governor [Arthur Phillip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Phillip) written by [Bennelong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bennelong) in 1796 is the first known work written in English by an Aboriginal person.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaher2013-318] Before that, Indigenous societies were [oral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_tradition). Their cultural isolation for most of the last 65,000 years proved a perfect situation for the construction and preservation of some of the world's oldest stories.[^cite_note-319] The historic [Yirrkala bark petitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yirrkala_bark_petitions) of 1963 are the first traditional Aboriginal documents recognised by the [Australian Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Parliament).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENAA_–_DaD-320]

In the 20th century, [David Unaipon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Unaipon) (1872–1967), known as the first Aboriginal author, is credited for providing the first accounts of Aboriginal mythology written by an Aboriginal person, in his *Legendary Tales of the Aborigines* (1924–1925). [Oodgeroo Noonuccal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oodgeroo_Noonuccal) (1920–1993)[^cite_note-321] was a famous Aboriginal poet, writer and rights activist, credited with publishing the first book of verse by an Aboriginal author, *We Are Going* (1964).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEModern_Australian_poetry-322] [Sally Morgan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sally_Morgan_\(artist\))'s novel *[My Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/My_Place_\(book\))* (1987) was considered a breakthrough memoir in terms of bringing Indigenous stories to a wider audience. The talents of playwrights [Jack Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Davis_\(playwright\)) and [Kevin Gilbert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Gilbert_\(author\)) were recognised. [Poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poetry) by Indigenous poets, including traditional song-poetry – ranging from sacred to everyday – has been published since the late 20th century.[^cite_note-323]

Writers coming to prominence in the 21st century include [Alexis Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis_Wright); [Kim Scott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Scott) (twice winner of the [Miles Franklin Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miles_Franklin_Award)); [Tara June Winch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tara_June_Winch); [Melissa Lucashenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melissa_Lucashenko); playwright and comedy writer [Nakkiah Lui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakkiah_Lui); in poetry [Yvette Holt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yvette_Holt); and in popular fiction [Anita Heiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anita_Heiss). Leading activists [Marcia Langton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcia_Langton), who wrote *[First Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Australians)* (2008) and [Noel Pearson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noel_Pearson) (*Up From the Mission*, 2009) are as of 2020 active contemporary contributors to Australian literature. Journalist [Stan Grant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stan_Grant_\(journalist\)) has written several non-fiction works on what it means to be Aboriginal in contemporary Australia, and [Bruce Pascoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Pascoe) has written both fiction and non-fiction works. [AustLit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AustLit)'s *BlackWords* project provides a comprehensive listing of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Writers and Storytellers. The [Living Archive of Aboriginal Languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Living_Archive_of_Aboriginal_Languages) contains stories written in traditional languages of the [Northern Territory](21638.md).

### Film and television

|  | It has been suggested that this section be [**split**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Splitting) out into another article titled *[Indigenous Australians in the performing arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Australians_in_the_performing_arts?action=edit\&redlink=1)*. ([**Discuss**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Indigenous_Australians)) *(January 2020)* |
| --- | --- |

See also: [Category\:Indigenous Australian mass media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Indigenous_Australian_mass_media), [Category\:Films about Aboriginal Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Films_about_Aboriginal_Australians), and [Cinema of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Australia)

Australian cinema has a long history, and the ceremonies of Indigenous Australians were among the first subjects to be filmed in Australia – notably a film of Aboriginal dancers in Central Australia, shot by the anthropologist [Baldwin Spencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Baldwin_Spencer) and [F.J. Gillen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F.J._Gillen) in 1900–1903.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMulvaney1990-324]

*[Jedda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jedda)* (1955) was the first Australian feature film to be shot in [colour film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colour_film), the first to star Aboriginal actors in lead roles ([Ngarla Kunoth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ngarla_Kunoth) and [Robert Tudawali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Tudawali)), and the first to be entered at the [Cannes Film Festival](101460.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFestival_de_Cannes2011-325] 1971's *[Walkabout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walkabout_\(film\))* was a British film set in Australia; it was a forerunner to many Australian films related to indigenous themes and introduced [David Gulpilil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Gulpilil) to cinematic audiences. *[Chant of Jimmie Blacksmith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Chant_of_Jimmie_Blacksmith_\(film\))* (1976), directed by [Fred Schepisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fred_Schepisi), was an award-winning historical drama from a book by [Thomas Keneally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Keneally), about the tragic story of an Aboriginal [bushranger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bushranger). [Peter Weir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Weir)'s 1977 mystery drama *[The Last Wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Last_Wave)*, also starring Gulpilil and featuring elements of Aboriginal beliefs and culture, won several [AACTA Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AACTA_Awards).

The canon of films related to Indigenous Australians increased from the 1990s, with [Nick Parson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nick_Parson?action=edit\&redlink=1)'s film *[Dead Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dead_Heart_\(film\))* (1996) featuring [Ernie Dingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernie_Dingo) and [Bryan Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryan_Brown);[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEASO_–_Dead_Heart-326] [Rolf de Heer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolf_de_Heer)'s *[The Tracker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Tracker_\(2002_film\))* (2002), starring [Gary Sweet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gary_Sweet) and David Gulpilil;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEASO_–_The_Tracker-327] and [Phillip Noyce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phillip_Noyce)'s *[Rabbit-Proof Fence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabbit-Proof_Fence_\(film\))* (2002).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEASO_–_Rabbit-Proof_Fence-328]

The [soundtrack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soundtrack) of the 2006 film *[Ten Canoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ten_Canoes)* directed by [Rolf de Heer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolf_de_Heer) was filmed entirely in dialects of the [Yolŋu Matha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yol%C5%8Bu_Matha) language group, with the main version featuring subtitles and English narration by David Gulpilil. The film won the [Un Certain Regard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Un_Certain_Regard) Special Jury Prize at the [2006 Cannes Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Cannes_Film_Festival).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaddoxAAP2006-329][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFestival_de_Cannes2006-330] *[The Straits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Straits)*, a 2012 drama series for TV based on an idea by Torres Strait Islander actor [Aaron Fa'aoso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Fa'aoso), was partly filmed in the Torres Strait Islands and starred Fa'aoso and [Jimi Bani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimi_Bani) (from [Mabuiag Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mabuiag_Island)), as well as [Papua New Guinean](22943.md) actors.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESwift2011-331] The documentary TV series *[Blue Water Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Water_Empire)* (aired 2019), featuring Fa'aoso and Bani, tells the story of Torres Strait Islands from [pre-colonial era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonisation_of_Australia) up to contemporary times.

Many Indigenous actors, directors, producers and others have been involved in the production of film and [TV series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_series) in the 21st century: [Ivan Sen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Sen), [Rachel Perkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachel_Perkins) (with her company [Blackfella Films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blackfella_Films)), [Aaron Pedersen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Pedersen), [Deborah Mailman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deborah_Mailman), [Warwick Thornton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warwick_Thornton), [Leah Purcell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leah_Purcell), [Shari Sebbens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shari_Sebbens), [Sally Riley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sally_Riley_\(producer\)), [Luke Carroll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luke_Carroll) and [Miranda Tapsell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miranda_Tapsell), [Wayne Blair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayne_Blair_\(director\)), and [Trisha Morton-Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trisha_Morton-Thomas), among others, with many of them well-represented in award nominations and wins.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEScreen_Australia2018-332] The films *[Sweet Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_Country_\(2017_film\))* (2017), [Top End Wedding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_End_Wedding) (2019) and TV series *[Cleverman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleverman_\(TV_series\))* and *[Total Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_Control_\(TV_series\))* (2019), all made by Aboriginal film-makers and featuring Aboriginal themes, were well-received and in some cases won awards.

The third series of the [sketch comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sketch_comedy) TV series *[Black Comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Comedy_\(TV_series\))*, co-written by [Nakkiah Lui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakkiah_Lui), [Adam Briggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Briggs), [Steven Oliver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steven_Oliver_\(Australian_actor\)?action=edit\&redlink=1) and others, and featuring many Indigenous actors, began to air in January 2020.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlack_Comedy-333]

### Theatre

Further information: [Theatre of Australia § Indigenous theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theatre_of_Australia#Indigenous_theatre)

## Recreation and sport

Main article: [Indigenous Australian sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Australian_sport)



1857 depiction of the [Jarijari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarijari) (Nyeri Nyeri) at [Mondellimin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mondellimin) engaged in recreational activities, including a type of Aboriginal football from the [Blandowski expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blandowski_expedition)

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlandowski1862-334]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWDL_–_Blandowski1862-335]

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHilferti201079-336]

Though lost to history, many traditional forms of recreation were played and while these varied from tribe to tribe, there were often similarities. Ball games were quite popular and played by tribes across Australia, as were games based on use of weapons. There is extensive documented evidence of traditional football games being played. Perhaps the most documented is a game popularly played by tribes in western [Victorian](4689460.md) regions of the [Wimmera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wimmera), [Mallee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mallee_\(Victoria\)) and [Millewa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millewa) by the [Djab wurrung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Djab_wurrung), [Jardwadjali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jardwadjali) and [Jarijari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarijari) people. Known as [Marn Grook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marn_Grook), it was a type of kick and catch football game played with a ball made of [possum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phalangeridae) hide.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFarnsworth2007-337] According to some accounts, it was played as far away as the [Yarra Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarra_Valley) by the [Wurundjeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wurundjeri) people,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllenderChristiansen200145-338] [Gippsland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gippsland) by the [Gunai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunai_people) people, and the [Riverina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Riverina) in south-western [New South Wales](21654.md). Some historians claim that Marn Grook had a role in the formation of [Australian rules football](2403.md), and many Aboriginal people, from children in remote communities to professional players at the highest level, the [Australian Football League](4732785.md), play the modern game. Well-known players include [Graham Farmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graham_Farmer), [Gavin Wanganeen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gavin_Wanganeen), [Adam Goodes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Goodes) and [Lance Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lance_Franklin). Goodes was also the [Australian of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_of_the_Year) for 2014.



Aboriginal cricket team with [Tom Wills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Wills) (coach and captain), [Melbourne Cricket Ground](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melbourne_Cricket_Ground), December 1866

A team of Aboriginal cricketers from the Western District of Victoria [toured England in 1868](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Aboriginal_cricket_team_in_England_in_1868), making it the first Australian sports team to travel overseas. Cricketer and [Australian rules football](2403.md) pioneer [Tom Wills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Wills) coached the team in an Aboriginal language he learnt as a child, and [Charles Lawrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Lawrence_\(cricketer\)) accompanied them to England. [Johnny Mullagh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnny_Mullagh), the team's star player, was regarded as one of the era's finest batsmen.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENMoA_–_cricket-339]

[Evonne Goolagong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evonne_Goolagong) became the world number-one ranked female [tennis](29773.md) player, with 14 [major tennis](197638.md) titles. [Ashleigh Barty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ashleigh_Barty), inspired by Goolagong, also reached number-one and won three major singles titles. Sprinter [Cathy Freeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathy_Freeman) earned gold medals in the [Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympics), World Championships, and [Commonwealth Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_Games). [Lionel Rose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lionel_Rose) earned a world title in [boxing](4243.md). [Arthur Beetson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Beetson), [Laurie Daley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurie_Daley) and [Gorden Tallis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorden_Tallis) captained Australia in [rugby league](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rugby_league), while [Mark Ella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Ella) captained Australia in [rugby union](25405.md). [Nathan Jawai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathan_Jawai) and [Patty Mills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patty_Mills) have played in the [National Basketball Association](22093.md).

Sporting teams include the [Indigenous All-Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_All-Stars_\(Australian_football\)), [Flying Boomerangs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flying_Boomerangs) and [Indigenous Team of the Century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Team_of_the_Century) in Australian rules football, and the [Indigenous All Stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_All_Stars_\(rugby_league\)), [NSW Koori Knockout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NSW_Koori_Knockout) and the [Murri Rugby League Team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murri_Rugby_League_Team) in rugby league.

## Contemporary issues

### Closing the Gap

Main article: [Closing the Gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closing_the_Gap)

To this day, the forced removal of children known as the [Stolen Generations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_Generations) has had a huge impact on the psyche, health and well-being of Indigenous Australians; it has seriously impacted not only the children removed and their parents, but their descendants as well. Not only were many of the [children abused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_abuse) – [psychologically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_abuse), [physically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_abuse), or [sexually](7397019.md) – after being removed and while living in group homes or adoptive families, but were also deprived of their culture alongside their families.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together:_Stolen_Generations-218] This has resulted in the disruption of [oral culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_history), as parents were unable to communicate their knowledge to their children, and thus much has been lost.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together:_Stolen_Generations-218]

There are many issues facing Indigenous people in Australia today when compared with the non-Indigenous population, despite some improvements. Several of these are interrelated, and include health (including shorter [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy) and higher rates of [infant mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality)), lower levels of education and employment, inter-generational trauma, high [imprisonment rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Australians_and_crime), [substance abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Substance_abuse) and lack of political representation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together:_Indigenous_disadvantage-340]

Some demographic facts are related to these issues, as cause or result:

- In the [2016 Australian Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Australian_Census), over 33% of the Indigenous population lived in major cities, compared with about 75% of the non-Indigenous population, with a further 24% in "inner regional" areas (compared with 18%), 20% in "outer regional" (8%), while nearly 18% lived in "remote" or "very remote" areas (2%).

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_3238.02018-259]

- The Indigenous population of Australia is much younger than the non-Indigenous population, with an estimated [median age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median_age) of 21 years (37 years for non-Indigenous), due to higher rates of birth and death.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_4704.02008-341]

  &#x20;For this reason, [age standardisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_adjustment) is often used when comparing Indigenous and non-Indigenous statistics.

  [^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_1301.02005a-342]

The federal government's [Closing the Gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closing_the_Gap) strategy, created in 2008 and coordinated by the [National Indigenous Australians Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Indigenous_Australians_Agency) since July 2019, aims to address multiple areas to improve the lives of Indigenous peoples. Draft targets for 2019 were created by the [Council of Australian Governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Australian_Governments) (COAG) in December 2018. These were in the following areas:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENIAA2018-343]

- families, children and youth

- health

- education

- economic development

- housing

- justice (including [youth justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juvenile_detention_in_the_Northern_Territory))

- land and water, "where Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples' land, water and cultural rights are realised"

- cross-system priorities, which "addresses racism, discrimination and social inclusion, healing and trauma, and the promotion of culture and language for Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander peoples"

### Health

See also: [Indigenous health in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_health_in_Australia)

Social and cultural determinants such as [discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism), lack of education or employment (and therefore [income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income)), and cultural disconnection can impact both physical and mental health, and contemporary disadvantage is related to colonisation and its ongoing impact.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together:_Indigenous_disadvantage-340][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Indigenous_HealthInfoNet2019-344]

Successive censuses have shown, that (after adjusting for demographic structures) Indigenous Australians experience greater rates of [renal disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renal_disease), several [communicable diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communicable_diseases) (such as [tuberculosis](30653.md) and [hepatitis C virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hepatitis_C_virus)), [type 2 diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Type_2_diabetes), [respiratory disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_disease), poor [mental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_health) and other illnesses than the general population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Indigenous_HealthInfoNet2019-344][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_1301.02005a-342]

#### Life expectancy

The life expectancy of Indigenous Australians is difficult to quantify accurately. Indigenous deaths are poorly identified, and the official figures for the size of the population at risk include large adjustment factors. Two estimates of Indigenous life expectancy in 2008 differed by as much as five years.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''National_Indigenous_Times''2008-345] The ABS introduced a new method in 2009,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_3302.0.55.003_–_Life_Tables2009-346] but problems remained. A 2013 study, referring to the national Indigenous reform policy, Closing the Gap, looked at the difficulties in interpreting the extent of the gap because of differing methods of estimating life expectancy between 2007 and 2012.[^cite_note-347] The 2019 report by the Close the Gap campaign reported that the gap in life expectancy was "widening rather than closing".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAHRC2019-348] Life expectancy for Aboriginals and Torres Strait islanders was 71.6 for men and 75.6 for women as of 2016–2017.

Infant mortality (ages 0–4) was twice as high as for non-Indigenous children in 2014–2016.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together:_Indigenous_disadvantage-340]

#### Mental health

[Mental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_health), [suicide](16831059.md) and [self-harm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-harm) remain major concerns, with the suicide rate being double that of the non-Indigenous population in 2015, and young people experiencing rising rates of mental health difficulties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together:_Indigenous_disadvantage-340] There are high incidences of [anxiety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anxiety), [depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_depressive_disorder), [PTSD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PTSD) and suicide amongst the [Stolen Generations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_Generations), with this resulting in unstable parenting and family situations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralians_Together:_Indigenous_disadvantage-340]

#### Substance abuse

Many Indigenous communities suffer from a range of health, social and legal problems associated with [substance abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Substance_abuse) of both legal and illegal drugs, including but not limited to [alcohol abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_abuse), [petrol sniffing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrol_sniffing), the use of illegal drugs such as [methamphetamine](11038318.md), [cannabis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cannabis), and [smoking tobacco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoking_tobacco).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Indigenous_HealthInfoNet2019-344] Tobacco use has been estimated to be the "greatest contributor (23%) to the gap in the disease burden between Indigenous and non-Indigenous Australians", with Indigenous people 2.5 times more likely to smoke daily than non-Indigenous Australians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAG.DoH2017-349]

Indigenous Australians were 1.6 times more likely to [abstain completely from alcohol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teetotaller) than non-Indigenous people in 2012–2013. [Foetal alcohol syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foetal_alcohol_syndrome) has been a problem, but the rate of pregnant women drinking had halved between 2008 and 2015 (from 20% to 10%).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Indigenous_HealthInfoNet2019-344]

[Petrol sniffing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrol_sniffing_in_Australia) has been a problem among some remote communities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELAoNT2004-350] A 2018 [longitudinal study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longitudinal_study) by the [University of Queensland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Queensland) (UQ), commissioned by the [National Indigenous Australians Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Indigenous_Australians_Agency),[^cite_note-351] reported that the number of people sniffing petrol in the 25 communities studied had declined by 95.2%, from 453 to just 22, related to the distribution of a new, low aromatic petrol, [Opal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opal_\(fuel\)), in NT in 2005.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Government2006b-352][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDovetail2019-353][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEd'AbbsGillickHodsonKavanagh2019-354][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinisters_Media_Centre2019c-355]

The 2018 UQ study also reported that alcohol and cannabis were the drugs causing most concern. [Ice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal_meth) was reported present in 8 of the 25 communities, but nearly all only occasional use.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEd'AbbsGillickHodsonKavanagh2019-354]

### Education

Further information: [Closing the Gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closing_the_Gap) and [National Sorry Day § Close the Gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Sorry_Day#Close_the_Gap)

There is a significant gap between Indigenous and non-Indigenous people in educational attainment. This presents significant issues for employment. As of 2018, Indigenous students or adults, when compared with non-Indigenous peers:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECtG_Report2019-356]

- Have a lower school attendance rate, with these rates at 82% and 93% respectively (in remote areas, as low as 63%)

- Have lower literacy and numeracy, although rates had improved significantly on some [NAPLAN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAPLAN) (standardised school testing) measures

- Reach [Year 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Year_12) at a lower rate, with improvement from 59% to 74% between 2006 and 2016, with the gap at 24% in 2016

- Are underrepresented in [higher education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education) and have lower completion rates

Closing the Gap has focused on improving education for Indigenous people, with some success. Attainment of Year 12 or equivalent for ages 20–24 has increased from 47.4% in 2006 to 65.3% in 2016. This has led to more Indigenous people undertaking higher or [vocational education](56551.md) courses. According to the *Closing the Gap* report, Indigenous students in higher education award courses more than doubled in number over the decade from 2006 (9,329) to 2017 (19,237).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECtG_Report2019-356]

However, most of the Closing the Gap targets for education are not on track. In general, the gaps have improved (such as in NAPLAN results) or not devolved (school attendance rate remaining stable for several years) have not met targets. Remoteness seems to be a factor; students in isolated or remote communities do not perform or attend as well as students in urban areas.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOPMC_Closing_the_Gap-357] The *Closing the Gap Report 2019* reported that of the seven targets, only two – early childhood education and Year 12 attainment – had been met. Only Year 9 numeracy was on track in all states and territories, with variations among them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECtG_Report2019-356]

The [Aboriginal Centre for the Performing Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_Centre_for_the_Performing_Arts) was established as a training centre by the state and federal governments in 1997.

### Employment

Compared to the national average, Indigenous people experience high unemployment and poverty rates. As of the *2018 Closing the Gap Report*, the Indigenous employment rate had decreased from 48% to 46.6% between 2006 and 2016, while the non-Indigenous employment rate remained steady at around 72% (a 25.4% gap). The employment rate for Indigenous women, however, increased from 39% to 44.8% in the same period.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEIAA2018-358]

The Racial Discrimination Act was introduced in 1975, and the Anti-Discrimination Act, in 1977. Legal action alone cannot prevent all forms of racism, however, these Acts were put into law to place checks on the system.[^cite_note-359]

A 2016 ABS report on labour force characteristics show low employment rates.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_4714.02016-360] An analysis of the figures suggested significant barriers to Indigenous people gaining employment, possibly including job location, employer discrimination, and lack of education and others. A big factor is education. Those with a [degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_degree) had an employment probability of 85% (for males) and 74% (for females) for gaining employment, decreasing along with qualifications, so that those who have completed Year 9 and below have a 43% (male) and 32% (female) probability of gaining employment. Other factors, unlike education, are not covered by government policy, such as discrimination and unfair treatment. Employed Indigenous Australians were more likely to experience discrimination than those who are unemployed, and it has been found that the second most common source of unfair treatment (after members of the public) is at work or applying for work. There was also a significant lack of consultation with Indigenous peoples on the methods they think best to tackle issues like unemployment.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBiddleHunterYapGray-361]

### Crime

Main article: [Indigenous Australians and crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Australians_and_crime)

Indigenous Australians are over-represented in Australia's criminal justice system. As of September 2019, Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander prisoners represented 28% of the total adult prisoner population,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS2019-362] while accounting for 3.3% of the general population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS2016-249] In May 2018, Indigenous women made up 34% of all women imprisoned in Australia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAHRC2018-363] A 2017–2018 report into youth justice undertaken by the [Australian Institute of Health and Welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Institute_of_Health_and_Welfare) reported that about half (a total of 2,339) of the young people aged 10–17 under supervision in 2016–2017 were Indigenous, although of that age group, Indigenous youth represent 5% of the general population. It concludes from the data that there is a clear issue occurring not only within Australia's [criminal justice system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_justice_system), but within communities as a whole.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAIHW_Youth_Justice2018-364]

Explanations given for this over-representation include the economic position of Indigenous Australians, the knock-on effects of the [stolen generations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_Generations) and disconnection from land, the effects of their health and housing situations, their ability to access an economic base such as land and employment, their education, and the use of alcohol and other drugs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEALRC:_incarceration_rate2018-365][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorff2021b-366]

Indigenous Australians are also over-represented as [victims of crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victims_of_crime), in particular, [assault](1466.md). Indigenous women are highly over-represented in this figure, accounting for a higher proportion of assault victims than the non-Indigenous category.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABS_4510.02017-367]

In 2007, the [Northern Territory Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Territory_Government) commissioned a [Board of Inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Board_of_Inquiry) into the Protection of Aboriginal Children from Sexual Abuse, which produced a report known as the *[Little Children are Sacred](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Children_are_Sacred)* report. This suggested, based largely on [anecdotal evidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anecdotal_evidence), that children in remote Aboriginal communities in NT were suffering from widespread [sexual abuse](7397019.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAmpe_Akelyernemane_Meke_Mekarle2007-368] The [Australian Human Rights Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Human_Rights_Commission)'s *Social Justice Report 2008* said that the 2005–2006 ABS statistics did not appear to support the "allegations of endemic child abuse... that was the rationale for the [NTER](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Territory_National_Emergency_Response)" ("The Intervention" by the [Howard government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_government)) that followed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAHRC2008-369]

### Family violence

See also: [Domestic violence in Australia § Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence_in_Australia#Indigenous_Australians)

The rate of family violence in Indigenous Australian communities, especially in the Northern Territory, has been high for many years, and under-reported.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAustralian_Institute_of_Health_and_Welfare2006-370] It has been estimated to be around 34 times greater than the national rate, and, in the worst areas, up to 80 times.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESpinney2016-371] There is no single cause for this high rate, but several probable causes or aggravating factors have been suggested by various researchers and stakeholders, including: dispossession of land and subsequent displacement of communities; childhood abuse experienced by the [Stolen Generations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stolen_Generations), along with [intergenerational trauma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intergenerational_trauma); economic [disadvantage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disadvantage); violent family environments; poor health; inadequate housing; [racism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racism_in_Australia); loss of [Aboriginal identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_Australian_identity); and many others.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKorff2022-372] An AIHW survey covering eight years to 2019, published in December 2021, revealed that Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander people accounted for 28 per cent of all hospitalisations due to family violence, despite only making up 3.3% of the total population. Various reasons were suggested by experts, including Aboriginal men's control of decision-making, and limited independence for women owing to economic factors; barriers in access to services; racism by some police and other services; and lack of enough Aboriginal-run organisations providing culturally safe services.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEConvery2021-373]

As the federal government, upon being urged by experts to create the means to halt the violence in 2021,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaydar2021-374] announced an extra A\$10.7 million "to boost frontline services in the Northern Territory... and to work towards our [Closing the Gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Closing_the_Gap) commitments", in addition to other funding already committed to the states and NT under the National Partnership on Family, Domestic and Sexual Violence Responses.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDoSSM2022-375]

## Efforts towards recognition and reparations

In 2021, Australia's government, led by Prime Minister [Scott Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Morrison), announced the creation of a reparations fund for members of the Stolen Generations—Indigenous Australians who were forcibly removed from their homes as children. This policy of forced assimilation, which continued into the 1970s, has had lasting impacts on Indigenous communities. The reparations fund includes one-off payments of 75,000 Australian dollars to victims, as part of a broader initiative to address the serious disadvantages faced by Australia's Indigenous population.[^cite_note-376]

In addition, there's an emerging solidarity between Black and Indigenous communities for finding land justice and reparations. Initiatives like the Indigenous-led Land Back movement and various Black community organizations are working towards reclaiming land and advocating for financial restitution for civil and human rights violations. These movements utilize cooperative structures to advance land justice, emphasizing the importance of communal wealth and regenerative systems that are not extractive of people or the planet.[^cite_note-377]

## Political issues

|  | It has been suggested that this section be [**split**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Splitting) out into another article titled *[Indigenous Australian politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Australian_politics)*. ([**Discuss**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Indigenous_Australians)) *(January 2020)* |
| --- | --- |

### Timeline

Since the 20th century there have been a number of individuals and organisations who have instigated significant events in the struggle for political representation, land rights and other political issues affecting the lives of Indigenous Australians:[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChrysanthos2019-378]

- 1937: [Yorta Yorta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yorta_Yorta) man [William Cooper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Cooper_\(Aboriginal_Australian\)) collects 1800 signatures to petition [King George VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_George_VI) for representation of the original occupants of Australia in federal Parliament.

- 26 January ([Australia Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_Day)) 1938: The [Aborigines Progressive Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aborigines_Progressive_Association) holds a [Day of Mourning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Day_of_Mourning_\(Australia\)), to protest 150 years of callous treatment and the seizure of land.

### Political representation

See also: [Uluru Statement from the Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uluru_Statement_from_the_Heart), [List of Indigenous Australian politicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indigenous_Australian_politicians), and [List of Indigenous Australians in politics and public service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indigenous_Australians_in_politics_and_public_service)

Under Section 41 of the [Australian Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Australia), Aboriginal Australians always had the legal right to vote in Australian Commonwealth elections if their State granted them that right. This meant that all Aboriginal peoples outside Queensland and Western Australia had a legal right to vote. The right of Indigenous ex-servicemen to vote was affirmed in 1949 and all Indigenous Australians gained the unqualified right to vote in Federal elections in 1962.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAEC_–_milestones-195] Unlike other Australians, however, voting was not made compulsory for Indigenous people, and it was not until the repeal of [Section 127 of the Constitution of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_127_of_the_Constitution_of_Australia) following the [1967 referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Australian_referendum_\(Aboriginals\)) that Indigenous Australians were counted in the population for the purposes of distribution of electoral seats.

As of January 2020, six Indigenous Australians have been elected to the [Australian Senate](165705.md): [Neville Bonner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neville_Bonner) (Liberal, 1971–1983), [Aden Ridgeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aden_Ridgeway) (Democrat, 1999–2005), [Nova Peris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_Peris) (Labor, 2013–2016), [Jacqui Lambie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacqui_Lambie) (2014–2017, 2019–*incumbent*), [Pat Dodson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Dodson) (Labor, 2016–*incumbent*), and former [Northern Territory](21638.md) MLA [Malarndirri McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malarndirri_McCarthy) (Labor, 2016–*incumbent*).

Following the [2010 Australian Federal Election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Australian_Federal_Election), [Ken Wyatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Wyatt) of the [Liberal Party](18453.md) won the Western Australian seat of Hasluck, becoming the first Indigenous person elected to the [Australian House of Representatives](61565.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKer2010-379][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBBC2010-380] His nephew, [Ben Wyatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Wyatt_\(politician\)), was concurrently serving as Shadow Treasurer in the [Western Australian Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Australian_Parliament) and in 2011 considered a challenge for the Labor Party leadership in that state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd2011-381] [Linda Burney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Burney) became the second Indigenous person, and the first woman, to serve in the federal House of Representatives.

In March 2013, [Adam Giles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Giles) of the [Country Liberal Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_Liberal_Party) (CLP) became [Chief Minister of the Northern Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_the_Northern_Territory) – the first Indigenous Australian to become head of government in a state or territory of Australia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC_News2013-382] [Hyacinth Tungutalum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyacinth_Tungutalum), also of the CLP, was the first Indigenous person elected to any Australian [(state *or* territory](4689096.md)) parliament. A [Tiwi man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiwi_people) from [Bathurst Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bathurst_Island_\(Northern_Territory\)), he was elected to the Northern Territory Legislative Assembly in October 1974 as the member for [Tiwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_division_of_Tiwi).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC:_death_of_Tungutalum2009-383]

A number of Indigenous people represent electorates at state and territory level, and [South Australia](26716.md) has had an Aboriginal Governor, Sir [Douglas Nicholls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Nicholls). The first Indigenous Australian to serve as a minister in any government was [Ernie Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernie_Bridge), who entered the Western Australian Parliament in 1980. [Carol Martin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carol_Martin_\(politician\)) was the first Aboriginal woman elected to a State parliament in Australia (the [Western Australian Legislative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Australian_Legislative_Assembly)) in 2001, and the first woman minister was [Marion Scrymgour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marion_Scrymgour), who was appointed to the Northern Territory ministry in 2002 (she became Deputy Chief Minister in 2008).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAEC_–_milestones-195] Representation in the Northern Territory has been relatively high, reflecting the high proportion of Aboriginal voters. The 2012 Territory election saw large swings to the conservative CLP in remote Territory electorates, and a total of five Aboriginal CLP candidates won election to the Assembly, along with one Labor candidate, in a chamber of 25 members. Among those elected for the CLP were high-profile activists [Bess Price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bess_Price) and [Alison Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alison_Anderson).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Australian''2012-384]

Forty people identifying as being of Indigenous Australian ancestry have been members of the ten Australian legislatures.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGobbett2017-207] Of these, 22 have been in the [Northern Territory Legislative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Territory_Legislative_Assembly). The [Northern Territory](21638.md) has an exceptionally high Indigenous proportion (about one third) of its population.
