[Adam Giles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Giles), who was [Chief Minister of the Northern Territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_Minister_of_the_Northern_Territory) from 2013 to 2016, was the first Indigenous head of government in Australia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC_News2013-382] In 1974, [the year of its creation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_Northern_Territory_general_election), the Northern Territory Legislative Assembly was also the first Australian parliament to have an Indigenous member elected to it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaensch1994-385]

The [2022 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Australian_federal_election) featured the largest number of Indigenous candidates in Australian history, with four running for the Coalition, eleven for Labor, and seventeen for the Greens.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECollard2022-386] The Greens Victorian senate ticket were all Aboriginal.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENicol2022-387][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorre2022-388][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEJeuniewic2022-389]

As of 2023, Indigenous Australians members of the senate represent 10.5% of the 76 Senate seats, and 1.9% in the House. The total representation is at 4.8%, far above the national population of 3.3%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEZaunmayr2022-390]

### Federal government initiatives

The [Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_and_Torres_Strait_Islander_Commission) (ATSIC) was set up as a representative body in 1990 under the [Hawke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Hawke) government. In 2004, the [Howard government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_government) disbanded ATSIC and replaced it and the Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Services (ATSIS) Regional and State Offices[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEATNS_–_ICC-391] with an appointed network of [Indigenous Coordination Centres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Coordination_Centres) (ICC) that administer Shared Responsibility Agreements and Regional Partnership Agreements with Aboriginal communities at a local level.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOIPC2006-392] ICCs operate as whole-of-government centres, housing staff from a number of departments to deliver services to Indigenous Australians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEATNS_–_ICC-391]

Major political parties in Australia have tried to increase Indigenous representation within their parties. One suggestion for achieving this is to introduce [seat quotas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reserved_political_positions), as in the [Maori electorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maori_electorates) in New Zealand.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd2009-393][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEO'Sullivan2017-394]

In October 2007, just before the calling of a [federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_Australian_general_election), the then Prime Minister, [John Howard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Howard), revisited the idea of bringing a referendum to seek recognition of Indigenous Australians in the Constitution (his government having previously sought to include recognition of Indigenous peoples in the [Preamble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preamble) to the Constitution in the [1999 Australian republic referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_Australian_republic_referendum)). His announcement was seen by some as a surprising adoption of the importance of the symbolic aspects of the reconciliation process, and reaction was mixed. The [Australian Labor Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party) initially supported the idea; however [Kevin Rudd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Rudd) withdrew this support just before the election, earning a rebuke from activist [Noel Pearson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noel_Pearson).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Australian''2007-395]

The [Gillard government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gillard_government) (2010–2013), with [bi-partisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bi-partisan) support, convened an [Expert Panel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expert_Panel_on_Recognising_Aboriginal_and_Torres_Strait_Islander_Peoples_in_the_Constitution) to consider changes to the [Australian Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Constitution) that would see recognition for Indigenous Australians, who delivered their report, which included five recommendations for changes to the Constitution as well as recommendations for the referendum process, in January 2012.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEANTaR2012-396][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEALII:_Summary_report2012-397] The Government promised to hold a referendum on the constitutional recognition of Indigenous Australians on or before the federal election due for 2013.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKarvelas2012-398] The plan was abandoned in September 2012, with Minister [Jenny Macklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jenny_Macklin) citing insufficient community awareness for the decision.

In December 2015, the 16-member Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Referendum Council was jointly appointed by the Prime Minister, [Malcolm Turnbull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_Turnbull), and Leader of the Opposition, [Bill Shorten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Shorten). After six months of consultation, the First Nations National Constitutional Convention met over four days from 23 to 26 May 2017, and ratified the *[Uluru Statement from the Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uluru_Statement_from_the_Heart)* by a [standing ovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_ovation) from the gathering of 250 Indigenous leaders. The *Statement* calls for a "First Nations Voice" in the Australian Constitution and a "Makarrata Commission"[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChrysanthos2019-378] (*Makarrata* is a [Yolngu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yolngu_language) word "describing a process of conflict resolution, peacemaking and justice").[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPearson2017-399]

#### 2019: Indigenous voice to government

See also: [Indigenous voice to government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_voice_to_government)

In May 2019, Prime Minister [Scott Morrison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Morrison) created the position of [Minister for Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minister_for_Indigenous_Australians), a [Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cabinet_of_Australia) portfolio in the [Second Morrison Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Morrison_Ministry), with Ken Wyatt as the inaugural officebearer.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENine_News2019-400][^cite_note-FOOTNOTENunnMao2019-401] On 30 October 2019, Wyatt announced the commencement of a "co-design process" aimed at providing an Indigenous voice to Parliament. The Senior Advisory Group is co-chaired by Professor [Tom Calma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Calma) [AO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Officer_of_the_Order_of_Australia), Chancellor of the [University of Canberra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Canberra), and Professor Dr [Marcia Langton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcia_Langton), Associate Provost at the [University of Melbourne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Melbourne), and comprises a total of 20 leaders and experts from across the country.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinisters_Media_Centre2019a-402][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMinisters_Media_Centre2019b-403] The first meeting of the group was held in Canberra on 13 November 2019.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWellington2019-404]

### Native title, sovereignty and treaties

Main articles: [Native title in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_title_in_Australia), [Aboriginal land rights in Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_land_rights_in_Australia), and [Indigenous treaties in Australia](61278660.md)

About 22% of land in Northern Australia ([Kimberley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kimberley_\(Western_Australia\)), [Top End](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Top_End) and [Cape York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_York_Peninsula)) is now Aboriginal-owned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEnviroNorthn.d.-405][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPEG2012-406] In the last decade, nearly 200 native title claims covering 1.3 million km<sup>2</sup> of land – approximately 18% of the Australian continent – have been approved.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeubauer2013-407]

In 1992, in *[Mabo v Queensland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mabo_v_Queensland_\(No._2\))*, the High Court of Australia recognised native title in Australia for the first time. The majority in the High Court rejected the doctrine of *terra nullius*, in favour of the concept of native title.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEALII1992-408]

In 2013, an Indigenous group describing itself as the [Murrawarri Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murrawarri_Republic) declared independence from Australia, claiming territory straddling the border between the states of New South Wales and Queensland.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeubauer2013-407] Australia's Attorney General's Department indicated it did not consider the declaration to have any meaning in law.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENeubauer2013-407]

In 2014, another Indigenous group describing itself as the [Sovereign Yidindji Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_Yidindji_Government) declared independence from Australia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowden2015-409]

Unlike in other parts of the former [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire), like the [Treaty of Waitangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Waitangi) in New Zealand, no treaty has ever been concluded between Indigenous Australians and an Australian government. However, although there is still no move toward a treaty at federal level, it is contended that the [Noongar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noongar) Settlement ([South West Native Title Settlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_West_Native_Title_Settlement)) in [Western Australia](33613.md) in 2016 constitutes a treaty, and at the state and territory levels there are currently (early 2018) other negotiations and preparatory legislation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHobbsWilliams2018-410] In [South Australia](26716.md), however, following the [2018 state election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_South_Australian_state_election) negotiations have been "paused".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWahlquist2018-411] In June 2018, the [Parliament of Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Victoria) passed a bill to advance the process of establishing a treaty with Aboriginal Victorians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOffer2018-412] The Victorian [First Peoples' Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Peoples'_Assembly) was elected in November 2019 and sat for the first time on 10 December 2019.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDunstan2019-413][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECostaDunstan2019-414]

## Prominent Indigenous Australians

Main article: [Lists of Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lists_of_Indigenous_Australians)



[Cathy Freeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathy_Freeman) surrounded by world media and carrying the [Aboriginal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aboriginal_flag) and [Australian flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_flag) following her victory in the 400 m final of the [Sydney Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney_Olympics), 2000

[ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Broadcasting_Corporation) footage and interviews of Australians celebrating Freeman's Olympics win – many noting how it brought the country together "as one"

After the arrival of European settlers in New South Wales, some Indigenous Australians became translators and go-betweens; the best-known was [Bennelong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bennelong), who eventually adopted European dress and customs and travelled to England where he was reportedly presented to [King George III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_George_III).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Canberra_Times''19884-415] Others, such as [Pemulwuy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pemulwuy), [Yagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yagan), and [Windradyne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windradyne), became famous for armed resistance to the European settlers.

During the twentieth century, as social attitudes shifted and interest in Indigenous culture increased, there were more opportunities for Indigenous Australians to gain recognition. [Albert Namatjira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Namatjira) became a painter, and actors such as [David Gulpilil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Gulpilil), [Ernie Dingo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernie_Dingo), and [Deborah Mailman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deborah_Mailman) became well known. Bands such as [Yothu Yindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yothu_Yindi), and singers [Christine Anu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christine_Anu), [Jessica Mauboy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jessica_Mauboy) and [Geoffrey Gurrumul Yunupingu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geoffrey_Gurrumul_Yunupingu), have combined Indigenous musical styles and instruments with pop/rock, gaining appreciation amongst non-Indigenous audiences. Polymath [David Unaipon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Unaipon) is commemorated on the [Australian \$50 note](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banknotes_of_the_Australian_dollar).

While relatively few [Indigenous Australians have been elected to political office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Indigenous_Australian_politicians) ([Neville Bonner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neville_Bonner), [Aden Ridgeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aden_Ridgeway), [Ken Wyatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Wyatt), [Nova Peris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nova_Peris), [Jacqui Lambie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacqui_Lambie) and [Linda Burney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Burney) remain the only Indigenous Australians to have been elected to the [Australian Federal Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Federal_Parliament)), Aboriginal rights campaigner Sir [Douglas Nicholls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Nicholls) was appointed Governor of the State of [South Australia](26716.md) in 1976, and many others have become famous through political activism – for instance, [Charles Perkins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_N._Perkins)' involvement in the [Freedom Ride](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Ride_\(Australia\)) of 1965 and subsequent work; or Torres Strait Islander [Eddie Mabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddie_Mabo)'s part in the landmark [native title](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_title) decision that bears his name. The voices of Cape York activists [Noel Pearson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noel_Pearson) and [Jean Little](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Aileen_Little), and academics [Marcia Langton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcia_Langton) and [Mick Dodson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mick_Dodson), today loom large in national debates. Some Indigenous people who initially became famous in other spheres – for instance, poet [Oodgeroo Noonuccal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oodgeroo_Noonuccal) – have used their celebrity to draw attention to Indigenous issues.

In health services, [Kelvin Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelvin_Kong) became the first Indigenous surgeon in 2006 and is an advocate of Indigenous health issues.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Australian''2008-416][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC2008-417][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEABC2003-418][^cite_note-FOOTNOTE''The_Sydney_Morning_Herald''2003-419]

[^cite_note-2]: &#x20;3.8% of Australia's population

[^cite_note-6]: &#x20;The use of the term Indigenous or Indigenous Australian is discouraged by many for being too generic or dehumanising.<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-4)</sup> Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islanders generally prefer more specific terms for their unique cultural origins or "Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander".<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAIATSIS2015-5)</sup>

[^cite_note-103]: &#x20;Genetics and material culture support repeated expansions into Paleolithic Eurasia from a population hub out of Africa, Vallini et al. 2022 (4 April 2022) Quote: "Taken together with a lower bound of the final settlement of Sahul at 37 ka (the date of the deepest population splits estimated by Malaspinas et al. 2016), it is reasonable to describe Papuans as either an almost even mixture between East Asians and a lineage basal to West and East Asians occurred sometimes between 45 and 38 ka, or as a sister lineage of East Asians with or without a minor basal OoA or xOoA contribution. We here chose to parsimoniously describe Papuans as a simple sister group of Tianyuan, cautioning that this may be just one out of six equifinal possibilities."

[^cite_note-130]: &#x20;Statistics compiled by Ørsted-Jensen for *Frontier History Revisited* (Brisbane 2011), page 10-11 & 15. Column one is the distribution percentage calculated on the estimates gathered and publicised in 1930 (Official Year Book of the Commonwealth of Australia XXIII, 1930, pp672, 687–696) by the social anthropologist Alfred Reginald Radcliffe-Brown. The percentage in column two was calculated on the basis of N.G. Butlin: *Our Original Aggression* and "others", by M. D. Prentis for his book *A Study in Black and White* (2 revised edition, Redfern NSW 1988, page 41). Column three however, is calculated on the basis of the "Aboriginal Australia" map, published by Australian Institute of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander Studies (AIATSIS), Canberra 1994.

[^cite_note-182]: &#x20;*Bringing them Home*, The general principle that came to be followed was that those who were identified as purely Aboriginal were left alone, because it was assumed that they would die out in a few generations, but part-Aboriginal people were "rescued" so that they could be brought up like white children. A few may have benefited from this, but for a majority of them separation from their families was distressing. [Appendices listing and interpretation of state acts regarding "Aborigines"](https://web.archive.org/web/20010113010900/http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/special/rsjproject/rsjlibrary/hreoc/stolen/stolen62.html): [Appendix 1.1 NSW](https://web.archive.org/web/20000817181808/http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/special/rsjproject/rsjlibrary/hreoc/stolen/stolen63.html); [Appendix 1.2 ACT](https://web.archive.org/web/20000817181819/http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/special/rsjproject/rsjlibrary/hreoc/stolen/stolen64.html); \[www\.austlii.edu.au/au/special/rsjproject/rsjlibrary/hreoc/stolen/stolen65.html Appendix 2 Victoria]; \[www\.austlii.edu.au/au/special/rsjproject/rsjlibrary/hreoc/stolen/stolen66.html Appendix 3 Queensland]; [Tasmania](https://web.archive.org/web/20000817181842/http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/special/rsjproject/rsjlibrary/hreoc/stolen/stolen67.html); \[www\.austlii.edu.au/au/special/rsjproject/rsjlibrary/hreoc/stolen/stolen68.html Appendix 5 Western Australia]; \[www\.austlii.edu.au/au/special/rsjproject/rsjlibrary/hreoc/stolen/stolen69.html Appendix 6 South Australia]; \[www\.austlii.edu.au/au/special/rsjproject/rsjlibrary/hreoc/stolen/stolen70.html Appendix 7 Northern Territory]&#x20;

[^cite_note-187]: &#x20;In its submission to the *Bringing Them Home* report, the Victorian government stated that "despite the apparent recognition in government reports that the interests of Indigenous children were best served by keeping them in their own communities, the number of Aboriginal children forcibly removed continued to increase, rising from 220 in 1973 to 350 in 1976" [*Bringing Them Home*: "Victoria".](https://web.archive.org/web/20000817181344/http://www.austlii.edu.au/au/special/rsjproject/rsjlibrary/hreoc/stolen/stolen10.html)

[^cite_note-193]: &#x20;Indigenous people across Australia and other colonist societies – Canada, New Zealand and South Africa – did not gain equal access to their repatriation benefits and military wages. In contrast to other Australian states, Aboriginal authorities in Victoria did not systematically deny Aboriginal people military allotments and pensions, but judged each case on its "merits" ([Horton 2015](#CITEREFHorton2015), p. 205).
